Quomodo etiam Sedes Romana de consensu cardinalium hoc approbavit?

*Quodsi dicas,* hoc non esse credendum, quod mendicitatem alicui imponat in *poenam,* vel quod approbet pro *regula* et vita sancta; sed[^p152-9] quod mendicitatem peregrinationis imponat, habetur de Poenitentia[^p152-10], ubi dicitur de sacerdote, qui revelat confes-

<!-- page 153 -->

siones, «quod deponatur et omni tempore vitae suae ignominiosus peregrinando pergat». Sed si viveret de labore honesto, vel de sumtibus propriis; non pergeret ut vilis, sed potius ut honorabilis. Restat igitur, quod imponit, ut vadat *ut mendicus.*

Quod autem pro *regula* approbet, patet per illud scriptum superius[^p153-1] allegatum, de Excessibus praelatorum, *Nimis prava,* ubi dicitur, quod «ordinem et regulam fratrum Praedicatorum et Minorum Sedes apostolica noscitur approbasse». Patet etiam per regulam beati Francisci a domino Honorio approbatam et confirmatam, ubi dicitur, quod fratres «vadant pro eleemosyna confidenter». Sed si erravit hoc approbando — et constat, eum hoc approbasse et confirmasse; constat etiam, quod universalis Ecclesia per totum mundum huiusmodi ordines et status acceptat — concludendum est ergo secundum dictum huius hominis unius, quod universalis Ecclesia tota erravit et decepta fuit; et qui huiusmodi statum erroneum invenerunt et approbaverunt, omnes damnati sunt; quod est horribilissimum et incredibilissimum, quod Deus permitteret sic[^p153-2] errare universaliter populum sanctum suum et tantam multitudinem sapientium, qui haec tempora praecesserunt. Quis autem hic tam *sapiens* et tam *iustus,* ut audeat ceteros praeter se iudicare fuisse deceptos et reprobos? Mira est haec *sapientia,* quae omnium ostendit insipientiam; et mira *iustitia,* quae ceteros condemnat. Quodsi hoc non est sapientia, sed potius temeritas, Sedem apostolicam velle iudicare, quae a solo Deo iudicatur, et eius iudicium et sententiam reprobare[^p153-3] et tot viros sanctos in infernum retrudere et in barathrum damnationis; procul ergo haec fiant a cordibus fidelibus et christianis.

V. Refellere etiam conatur plurima, immo fere omnia tanquam *doctrinae apostolicae inimica,* pro eo quod dicit, mendicationem ab Apostolo esse prohibitam tanquam malam et tantum in casu esse concessam. — Dicit etiam, me fuisse mentitum et contra Scripturam et Apostolum defendisse falsum et defendere, in hoc quod dicit, me dixisse, quod laborare opere manuali non sit praeceptum. Dicit igitur, quod *mendicare* non est indifferens, sed malum; et ideo propter adiectionem circumstantiae fieri nequaquam potest bonum; immo, quod plus est, in praedicantibus asserit esse simoniacum.

Hoc autem suae positionis fundamentum, frequentius iteratum, facile est advertere esse ruinosum cum omni eo, quod superaedificatur. Nam si *mendicare* prohibitum est, et *mendicare* peccare: ergo *esse mendicum* est *esse peccatorem.* Cum ergo Christus se dicat *mendicum,* immo Propheta[^p153-4] in persona Christi; fatetur ergo Christus, se esse peccatorem; quod dicere vel credere non parvum est flagitium vel peccatum. — *Rursus,* si *mendicare* peccare est, mendicitas sive mendicatio peccatum est. Sed secundae ad Corinthios octavo[^p153-5], super illud: *Ut eius inopia vos divites essetis;* Glossa dicit: «Nemo se contemnat: pauper in cella, dives in conscientia securior dormit in terra, quam auro dives in purpura. Non ergo expavescas cum tua mendicitate ad illum accedere, qui indutus est nostra paupertate; ubi se pauperavit, nos ditavit». Ergo mendicitas disponit, non retrahit a divino accessu; constat igitur, quod non est peccatum.

Amplius, Chrysostomus super Matthaeum homilia sexagesima nona in fine[^p153-6] dicit, quod «mendicare in nullo offendit Deum; furari autem sive rapere offendit Deum et hominem». Hoc etiam patet per auctoritatem Chrysostomi superius allegatam de homilia quarta super Matthaeum. Non ergo est ab Apostolo prohibitum, nec invenitur alicubi, hoc Apostolum prohibuisse, sed potius cum suis coapostolis commendasse. Nam primae ad Corinthios decimo sexto[^p153-7] et praedicavit et procuravit, quod collectae fierent in pauperes Sanctorum, qui erant in Ierusalem, sicut eleemosynae eis gratis impensae. Et ad Galatas secundo dicit, quod *Petrus et Iacobus dexteras dederunt mihi et Barnabae societatis, ut nos in gentibus, ipsi in circumcisione, tantum ut pauperum memores essemus;* Glossa: «*Pauperum,* qui rerum suarum venditarum pretium ad pedes Apostolorum posuerant, *memores essemus,* ad opus illorum faciendo collectas». Si ergo Apostolus simoniacus non erat in hoc, quod collectas petebat et discipulos mittebat ad hoc, ut peterent eleemosynas ab his quibus spiritualia seminaverat, pro pauperibus, qui erant in Ierusalem; si summus Pontifex Ordines statuat pauperes, qui spiritualia seminent et de eorum quos aedificant verbo et exemplo eleemosynis gratuitis, petitis, seu ultro oblatis, vivant: ab Apostolo in nullo prorsus discordat, quin potius concordat, cum tan-

<!-- page 154 -->

tam, sicut ipse, et maiorem potestatem habeat in gubernando et disponendo ipsius Ecclesiae membra.

Sed *obiicies,* quod Apostolus praecepit laborem corporalem in textu, et Glossae[^p154-1] videntur dicere, quod hoc facit contra mendicitatem.

Sed ad hoc est plana responsio: esto, quod Apostolus laborem huiusmodi manualem praecipiat — quod nunquam negavi, sicut apparet in verbis meis, sed quod hoc non *universaliter* praecipit servis Christi, nisi forte in articulo necessitatis, in quo non patet licita via habendi victum nisi per laborem manuum — sed praecipit personis *determinatis,* secundum quod apparet ex textu[^p154-2]: *Audivimus quosdam inter vos ambulantes inquiete, nihil operantes, sed curiose agentes. His autem, qui eiusmodi sunt, denuntiamus et obsecramus in Domino Iesu Christo, ut cum silentio operantes suum panem manducent.* Et paulo ante: *Denuntiamus, ut subtrahatis vos* etc.; Glossa: «Incipit hic de curiosis et otiosis, monens, ut corrigantur». Patet ergo ex serie textus et ex Glossa, quod mandatum illud se extendit ad curiosos et otiosos. — Similiter primae ad Thessalonicenses quarto[^p154-3]: *Rogamus vos, fratres, ut operam detis, ut quieti sitis, et operemini manibus vestris, sicut praecepimus vobis;* Glossa: «*Operemini,* quod adiuvat vos, quietos esse. Illud enim malum de otio venit». Ex hoc etiam patet, quod hoc dicit ad otium vitandum et pigritiam tollendam, quae est sentina desideriorum et fomentum peccatorum. Unde ad Ephesios quarto[^p154-4]: *Qui furabatur iam non furetur, magis autem laboret operando manibus suis* etc. — Ex hoc igitur patet, quod labor non *omnibus* praecipitur; ac per hoc non inhibetur mendicatio, nisi illa quae venit ex otio et cupiditate, secundum quod insinuant textus et Glossae.

Sed *obiicies* de Augustino, de Opere monachorum, qui loquebatur ad monachos, qui spiritualibus exercitiis erant occupati et intenti, non otiosi; et illis dicit, quod ad huiusmodi opera sunt astricti.

Ad hoc apparet manifesta responsio, si attendatur conclusio circa finem, qua confutat eorum positionem, qui repudiabant operationem corporalem; in quo male sentiebant, ibi[^p154-5]: «Quamobrem illi qui, relicta sive ampla sive qualicumque opulentia, inter pauperes Christi vivere voluerunt, si corpore valent et ab ecclesiasticis occupationibus vacant; tamen si et ipsi manibus operantur, misericordius agunt, quam cum omnia sua indigentibus diviserunt. Quod si nolunt, quis audeat cogere? Illi autem, qui praeter istam sanctam societatem vitam labore corporis transigebant, si non laborant, non manducent».

Apparet igitur, qui sunt astricti, scilicet validi et fortes, et qui ab ecclesiasticis occupationibus vacant, et qui vitam in saeculo de labore corporis transigebant, et tales multi erant inter illos monachos, et plures erant[^p154-6] in exordio religionis Minorum fratrum, et ideo ad labores erant arctandi; nunc autem comparatione aliorum paucissimi sunt tales: et ideo non est contra Apostolum, si talibus conceditur simpliciter[^p154-7] mendicare, maxime cum talis actus non sit multum appetibilis nec delectabilis nec honorabilis, immo repletus multis vilitatibus et miseriis, ad quem nullius valentis viri cor posset inclinari nisi in spe praemii aeterni et amore Christi et desiderio proficiendi cum adiutorio gratiae Spiritus sancti, sine qua vix credo posset dives ad tantae abiectionis incommodum inclinari. Nec tales gravant populum Domini et Ecclesiam, maxime si nihil ultra necessaria petant, quia multo plura expenderent et consumerent de eleemosynis pauperum, si clerici in saeculo in beneficiis praebendalibus remansissent; nec tunc[^p154-8], quando modicum intenderent spiritualibus operibus, etiam in divitiis praecluderetur via salutis. Absit igitur, quod nunc praecludatur in tanta arctitudine paupertatis.

Ex iam dictis in hac quaestione de paupertate, ut cetera recolligamus in summa, apparet, quod sicut *mendicare* in pauperibus debilibus et infirmis est *necessitas naturae,* in validis otiosis et cupidis est *vitiositas culpae;* sic in pauperibus voluntariis, contemptoribus sui et imitatoribus Christi et praedicatoribus Evangelii est *actus consonans,* non repugnans *evangelicae perfectioni,* pro eo quod omnibus universaliter *renuntiare,* nullam rem sibi appropriando, est *consultum*[^p154-9] a Domino ut perfectum; eleemosynas *accipere* iustis pauperibus est *concessum* tanquam licitum et in nullo perfectionis evangelicae inimicum; eleemosynas etiam *humiliter petere* pauperibus voluntariis *non est inhibitum* tanquam malum; vitam suam de *lucro laboris sui transigere* non est omnibus validis pauperibus iniunctum tanquam praeceptum necessario astrictivum.

<!-- page 155 -->

Si quis ergo impugnat rerum temporalium *universalem abrenuntiationem,* impugnat Christum pauperem, crucifixum; impugnat evangelicum consilium; impugnat coetum Apostolorum dicentium[^p155-1]: *Ecce nos reliquimus omnia;* impugnat etiam Spiritum sanctum, qui hoc inspirat et suggerit cordibus perfectorum; impugnat et ipsum Deum Patrem universorum, qui est *refugium pauperum;* impugnat denique regnum caelorum, totum universum, cuius dominium pauperi est concessum, dicente Domino: *Beati pauperes spiritu, quoniam ipsorum est regnum caelorum;* et ideo contra talem *insensatum* necesse est ut *pugnet orbis terrarum.*

Qui autem impugnat *eleemosynarum acceptionem,* et Christum impugnat, qui stipendium accipiebat a mulieribus sibi servientibus et se invitantibus, Lucae septimo[^p155-2]; et coetum Apostolorum, qui et ipsi eleemosynas accipiebant, quae *ponebantur ad pedes eorum,* Actuum quarto; et statum monachorum, qui super eleemosynas fundati sunt; et universum clerum — nam et ipsi de eleemosynis vivunt, quae ultro ecclesiis Christi collatae sunt — et per consequens communem pauperum statum, quem tamen Scripturae divinae indicant Deo maxime esse acceptum.

Qui vero impugnat humilem *eleemosynarum petitionem* in eis qui egent et licite possunt accipere, impugnat *Christum,* qui humiliter petiit hospitium, qui etiam fatetur, se[^p155-3] esse mendicum; impugnat et *Paulum,* qui petiit, collectas fieri pauperibus Sanctis, et hoc de voluntate Petri; impugnat *vinculum caritatis,* quod facit communicari *in ratione dati et accepti;* impugnat *officium humanitatis,* per quam indigens exponit necessitatem suam, ne sibi sit inhumanus, et alium excitat, qui potest et vult, ut proximo sit humanus et pius; et in hoc nullus offenditur, nulli praeiudicatur, nihil inordinate concupiscitur, nihil indebite aufertur, in eis praecipue, qui non quaerunt *datum,* sed *fructum*[^p155-4].

Qui autem impugnat *otiositatem, cupiditatem, carnalitatem et superfluitatem* in ceteris Christianis et praecipue in religiosis, bene facit et recte, si tamen secundum ordinem rectum procedat.

Qui autem universaliter omnibus validis *onus imponit lucrativi laboris,* de quo debeant sustentari, praeceptum Apostoli nimis ampliat et iugum servitutis super Christi servos nimium aggravat et spirituales viros a spiritualibus occupationibus et laboribus retardat in periculum salutis animarum, dum magis facit eos sollicitos de suis morituris corporibus pascendis quam de proximorum animis Domino acquirendis. Quod si fiat, *decimatur mentha et cyminum, et relinquentur quae graviora sunt Legis, iudicium et misericordia*[^p155-5], praefertur misericordia corporalis spirituali; ac per hoc corpus spiritui praefertur, privatum commodum utilitati communi, quae specialiter consistit in aedificatione populi Christi, in eleemosynis, in documentis et animarum consiliis, ad quae misit Deus istos ordines postremos per summum Pontificem et praelatos. Ad quae si quis dicat eos *non missos,* manifeste obviat *veritati,* cum expressa ad hoc testimonia habeantur tam in regula quam in privilegio et institutione et approbatione huius duplicis ordinis[^p155-6]. — Si autem dicat, *non debere mitti;* detrahit *evangelicae perfectioni.* Quis enim magis idoneus ad hoc, quam qui devovit evangelicam perfectionem? — Si quis dicat, eos a Papa et episcopis *non posse mitti;* derogat *apostolicae auctoritati* et *potestati clavium* in praelatis et *plenitudini potestatis* in summo Pontifice, qui in terris, ut[^p155-7] Scriptura asserit, fides sentit, iura testantur, rationes etiam irrefragabiles convincunt, ut caput unum et summum et sponsus unicus et hierarcha praecipuus, in quo est totius Ecclesiae militantis status, obtinet locum Christi. Et ideo omne genu debet ei curvari, et principum et praelatorum et clericorum et laicorum et religiosorum in terris, ad instar Christi in caelis, cui *omne genu flectitur caelestium, terrestrium et infernorum*[^p155-8]. Amen.

---

## English
<!-- page 134 -->

### Article II. *On poverty as to mendicancy*[^p134-1].

Next the question is about mendicancy, and it is asked whether to beg for Christ belongs to Christian perfection; and I call *to beg* to seek alms, or to live on daily alms. And that it does not, it seems:

1. First, by the authority of the Psalm[^p134-2]: *Fear the Lord, all ye his saints: for there is no want to them that fear him:* therefore to be in want and to fear God do not stand together.

2. Likewise, the Psalm[^p134-3]: *I have not seen the just forsaken, nor his seed seeking bread:* therefore he who seeks bread is not just, nor the seed of the just.

3. Likewise, the Psalm[^p134-4]: *Let his children be carried about vagabonds, and beg;* but nothing that the Psalmist, speaking in the spirit of God, imprecates as a punishment pertains to evangelical perfection or to perfect justice; to beg is of this kind: therefore etc.

4. Likewise, Ecclesiasticus, chapter 40[^p134-5]: *My son, in thy life be not indigent; for it is better to die than to be indigent:* therefore it is better to be dead than a beggar.

5. Likewise, Acts, chapter 20[^p134-6]: *It is a more blessed thing to give, rather than to receive;* but to give alms does not make a man attain to evangelical perfection: therefore much less does receiving.

6. Likewise, Luke, chapter 12[^p134-7]: *Sell what you possess;* the Gloss: «that, having once spurned all things for God, you may afterwards work with the labour of your hands for what to live on, or to give alms»: therefore to live by one's own labour and to give alms pertains to evangelical perfection: therefore its opposite, namely to beg and to live by another's labour, is contrary to evangelical perfection.

7. Likewise, the first to the Thessalonians, chapter 4[^p134-8]: *Desire nothing of any man's;* the Gloss: «*Desire*

<!-- page 135 -->

*nothing of any man's,* much less ask for it, or take it». If therefore *to ask* or *to request* is more than *to crave,* and the first, namely *to desire* or to crave what is another's, is forbidden by the Apostle as unlawful: therefore much more strongly *to request.*

8. Likewise, the first to the Thessalonians, chapter 5[^p135-1]: «*We beseech you, that you know them who labour among you, and are over you in the Lord;* the Gloss: «Just as riches beget neglect of salvation, so need, while it longs to be sated, turns aside from justice»; but what makes one turn aside from justice does not pertain to evangelical perfection: therefore etc.

9. Likewise, the second to the Thessalonians, chapter 3[^p135-2]: *If any man will not work, neither let him eat;* the Gloss: «The Apostle wills that the servants of God work bodily, so that they may have whereon to live, and not be compelled by need to request necessities»; but this is what mendicants do: therefore it does not belong to the servants of God to beg, according to the Apostle.

10. Likewise, in the same chapter, on that text[^p135-3]: *That we might give a pattern unto you;* the Gloss: «He who frequently comes to another's table, given to idleness, must needs flatter the one who feeds him»; but to flatter is contrary to truth and to perfection: therefore etc.

11. Likewise, in Causa 12, question 1, *Videntes*[^p135-4], Pope Urban says that «bishops ought to minister all necessities to all who wish to live the common life, as best they can, so that no one among them be found needy». And afterwards: «If anyone shall arise, in modern or in future times, who strives to tear this away, let him be struck with the condemnation already stated»: therefore it is against the canonical sanctions that some should live the common life in the Church, being in need and begging in it.

12. Likewise, in *De consecratione*, distinction 1[^p135-5], it is forbidden that a church be consecrated unless it is first endowed: therefore, if a church ought not to be consecrated unless those who minister in it have whereby to be sustained, much more strongly ought no religious order to be instituted without a sustaining possession.

13. Likewise, in civil law it is forbidden that the able-bodied beg, by the law on able-bodied mendicants[^p135-6]; but the Church ought to be more ordered than the commonwealth: if therefore it is against the right governance of the commonwealth that the able-bodied beg, much more is it against the sublimity of evangelical perfection.

14. Likewise, this same thing is shown *by reason.* To beg is an indifferent work; but an indifferent [work] does not become a work of perfection through the addition of an end, as appears manifestly in other works, such as going to church and giving alms: therefore etc.

15. Likewise, it belongs to perfection to serve freely and liberally and to flee the obligations of sins; but he who receives alms obliges himself to intervene[^p135-7] for the sins of others: if therefore an obligation of this kind is not consonant with perfection, therefore etc.

16. Likewise, those of this kind who beg by profession do this either because they are *poor,* or because they are *preachers.* If because *poor:* then all the poor who beg are perfect, which is against Augustine in the book *On the Work of Monks*[^p135-8], who rebukes monks who beg and are willing to do no work. If because *preachers:* then no one among them ought to beg who does not preach; the contrary of which we see all day long.

17. Likewise, to preach is something spiritual; but he who gives a spiritual thing and receives a temporal one is like Giezi, who received money from Naaman on the pretext of a spiritual benefit previously shown to him[^p135-9]; but this is damnable: therefore it is damnable for a preacher to beg: therefore it does not belong to evangelical perfection.

18. Likewise, it belongs to the perfect to refrain *from every evil appearance*[^p135-10]; but this is an evil appearance, that preachers request temporal things, because in this they are like the pseudo-preachers: therefore etc.

<!-- page 136 -->

19. Likewise, those of this kind either have authority in preaching, or they do not. If they do not: then they do ill by preaching, therefore they merit nothing; if they have it — but those who have authority can request by right of their power: therefore such men, by begging[^p136-1], do injury to the authority of the preaching of the Gospel: therefore in this they are not perfect, but impious.

20. Likewise, it is safer and more perfect to follow men more approved and wiser; but the most approved and wisest men in the religious orders were Basil, Benedict, Augustine, Jerome, Gregory, Hilary and Paulinus; but such men so left their own things that they possessed things in common, nor are they read to have begged: therefore this mode of begging newly introduced by Dominic and Francis seems to be superstitious and vain, since it seems foolish to set the mind and spirit of two poor men before such great wisdom and sublimity of the Saints and doctors who went before[^p136-2].

Sᴇᴅ ᴄᴏɴᴛʀᴀ: 1. Matthew, chapter 10[^p136-3]: *Do not possess gold, nor silver, nor money in your purses, nor scrip for your journey, nor two coats, nor shoes;* the Gloss: «Let them not carry with them even necessary things, nor a store-room; nothing besides clothing, with which one can be content». But in this the form of perfection is handed down to the Apostles themselves, and to imitate what belongs to perfection is perfect: therefore so to go through the world pertains to evangelical perfection. But this is to beg and to live on alms: therefore this pertains to evangelical perfection.

2. Likewise, Mark, chapter 6[^p136-4]: *He commanded them that they should take nothing for the way, neither bread, nor money in their purse;* therefore he commanded this to them either as to *prelates,* or as to the *perfect.* If as to *prelates:* then prelates would still be bound to it; which is manifestly false. Therefore he commanded this to them as to perfect men; but perfection is set before all in common to be imitated, for it is said: *He that can take, let him take it:* therefore so to live pertains to evangelical perfection.

3. Likewise, Luke, chapter 9[^p136-5]: *Take nothing for your journey, neither staff, nor scrip, nor bread, nor money;* the Gloss: «*Not to carry a staff* is not to be solicitous about the things that are necessary for human sustenance, because these are owed to preachers»; if *therefore* the Lord urged upon the Apostles, whom he sent to preach even among *unbelievers,* the exclusion of solicitude about necessary sustenance, much more strongly, when the Gospel is preached to *the faithful,* does it belong to perfection not to be solicitous about such things, but to be content with the food and clothing given one.

4. Likewise, Luke, chapter 9[^p136-6]: *Whosoever shall receive a child in my name;* the Interlinear: «a poor man»; and another Gloss: «He who receives an imitator of Christ receives Christ»: therefore when a poor man imitating Christ is received, Christ is received. If therefore it belongs to evangelical perfection to be poor and an imitator of Christ, it belongs to evangelical perfection to wish to be received for Christ in such a poor man. If therefore such a one requests by begging, he does not withdraw from evangelical perfection.

5. Likewise, Luke, chapter 10[^p136-7]: *Carry neither purse, nor scrip;* the Gloss: «So great ought to be the preacher's confidence in God that, even if he does not provide beforehand the expenses of the present life, he should yet know most certainly that they will not fail him, lest, while his mind is occupied with temporal things, he preach eternal things the less». Therefore it makes for a more unencumbered preaching of the truth that one should not provide temporal things for oneself beforehand, but expect them to be given one by others when one has need: therefore such a mode of living is consonant with truth and with evangelical perfection.

6. Likewise, Luke, chapter 16[^p136-8]: *Make unto you friends of the mammon of iniquity; that when you shall fail, they may receive you into everlasting dwellings;* the Gloss: «Not any poor whatever, but those who can receive into the everlasting dwellings»; but such are *the poor*

<!-- page 137 -->

*in spirit, for theirs is the kingdom of heaven:* therefore it is according to the divine counsel that alms be given to such poor and that they be received by them. But what is consonant with the divine counsel is not repugnant to evangelical perfection: therefore etc.

7. Likewise, Jerome against Vigilantius[^p137-1], treating the aforesaid word, says: «Can these poor, amid whose filth and uncleanness of body burning lust holds sway, have the everlasting dwellings, who possess neither present things nor future ones? For not the poor simply, but the poor in spirit are called blessed, of whom it is written: *Blessed is he that understandeth concerning the needy and the poor: the Lord will deliver him in the evil day.* In sustaining the poor of the common people there is need not at all of understanding, but of alms; in the holy poor there is the blessedness of intelligence, that you should give to him who blushes to receive and, when he has received, grieves, reaping carnal things and sowing spiritual ones».

8. Likewise, the second to the Corinthians, chapter 8[^p137-2]: *Let your abundance supply their want, that their abundance also may be a supplement to your want;* the Gloss: «*Let your abundance* of earthly things *supply their want, that,* conversely, the *abundance* of the spiritual merits *of those* who are free for divine things[^tr-vacare] *may be a supplement to your want*». If therefore it pertains to the perfection of the Lord's body that the members communicate with one another *in the matter of giving and receiving,* it makes for the perfection of the Church and of the Gospel that the just poor request and receive sustenance from the worldly and imperfect rich. — *If you say* that they can *receive,* but not *request;* this is nothing, because the worldly rich can request the prayers of spiritual men to relieve the want of their own merits: therefore, conversely, the spiritual poor can request bodily alms for the sustenance of their bodies.

9. Likewise, this same thing is shown *by the example of Christ.* The Psalm[^p137-3]: *But I am a beggar and poor;* the Gloss: «Christ says this of himself: I am a beggar in the form of a servant. A *beggar* is he who requests from another, a *poor man* is he who is not sufficient for himself». But it is certain that this is not understood of Christ as to spiritual gifts or goods, because he was a comprehensor and full of all grace: therefore it is understood as to temporal goods; therefore as to these he was a beggar and requested from another.

10. Likewise, the Psalm[^p137-4]: *He persecuted the man in want and the beggar;* the Gloss: «namely Christ. To persecute the poor is cruelty alone; others indeed sometimes suffer such things for the sake of riches and honours».. Either therefore Christ is called a *beggar* because he begged from the *Father,* or because he begged from *man.* If because he begged from the *Father,* then all men are beggars, the rich as well as the poor: therefore so to persecute Christ would not have been the height of cruelty; if because he begged from *men,* we have the proposition: therefore etc.

11. Likewise, Mark, chapter 11[^p137-5]: *Having viewed all things round about, he went out to Bethania;* the Gloss: «[to see] whether anyone would receive him with hospitality; for he was of such great poverty, and so little the flatterer of anyone, that in so great a city he found no lodging»; but it is certain that, if he had wished to hire a lodging, he would have found one to hire: therefore he looked round about like a poor little man and a beggar.

12. Likewise, Luke, chapter 19[^p137-6]: *Zacheus, make haste and come down; for this day I must abide in thy house;* the Gloss: «Not invited, he invites»; but to invite another to this, that he receive one with hospitality, is nothing other than to beg and to request: therefore etc.

13. Likewise, John, chapter 4[^p137-7]: *Give me to drink;* Augustine in the original, the fifteenth homily on John: «In everything that Christ did as man, he was holding out to men who would believe in him

<!-- page 138 -->

an example»; and afterwards: «He *asks* for drink and *promises* drink; he is in need as one who will receive, and he promises as one who will satisfy». But it is certain that he was not in need of *spiritual* drink, because he was promising it: therefore he was in need of *bodily* drink and was asking for it. — But you will object the Gloss as if contrary, which says: «Although Jesus after his weariness could thirst, yet he does not demand this drink»; but this is understood to mean that he was not principally requesting this drink, lest the Gloss contradict the deed.

14. Likewise, Jerome to Rusticus[^p138-1]: «What is viler than the Samaritan woman? Yet she not only believes in the Messiah, whom she comes to know at the well, but also, while the Apostles are buying food, she refreshes him when hungry and sustains him when weary»: therefore it seems that Christ was sustained bodily by her.

15. Likewise, as the Gospel history relates[^p138-2], Christ, invited by the Pharisees, ate with them: either therefore as *lord,* or as a *poor man.* If as *lord:* then he did not give an example of poverty; if as a poor man — but this is to beg: therefore etc.

16. Likewise, for this there is *the example of the Saints.* In Hebrews, chapter 11[^p138-3], it is said in praise of the Saints: *They wandered about in sheepskins, in goatskins, being in need* etc.; but so to wander about is to beg: therefore etc.

17. Likewise, there is the example of Elias, the third of Kings, chapter 17[^p138-4], of whom Augustine says in a certain sermon: «Had God failed to feed his servant Elias? Did not birds minister to him who lacked men? God therefore showed that he can feed his servants whence he will and when he will; and yet, so that a religious widow might feed him, he made him to be in need».

18. Likewise, for this there is the example of the disciples of John the Evangelist, of whom it is said in his legend[^p138-5] that, selling all things that they could have, they gave to the needy; and afterwards it is added that they were tempted, because they had seen themselves in need in one cloak, but their own servants rich and resplendent».

19. Likewise, the example of the disciples of Paul; whence Chrysostom, on the praises of Paul[^p138-6]: «Paul did not possess money; for he himself also testifies to this: *Even unto this hour,* he says, *we both hunger and thirst, and are naked, and are buffeted, and have no fixed abode;* and why do I say money, when he indeed often had not even necessary food, nor clothing to put around him»? And there follows: «Of his disciples too, very many were poor, unskilled and strangers to all learning, living in hunger and need, obscure» etc.: therefore the disciples of Paul were beggars.

20. Likewise, for this there is the example of blessed Benedict, of whom it is read in the second book of the *Dialogues*[^p138-7] that for three years he was in a cave, doing no work at all, but a certain Roman monk ministered bread to him; and it is certain that Benedict did not sin in this.

21. Likewise, the example of Saint Alexius, who was a man of wonderful holiness, in whose legend[^p138-8] it is read that, being the son of a very wealthy Roman, he despised his patrimony and went begging through the world.

22. Likewise, the example of Saint Dominic[^p138-9], who at his death imprecated a curse on all who should try to bring possessions into his order: therefore he willed that they live on alms, according as he himself lived.

23. Likewise, for this there is the example of blessed Francis, who not only begged and counselled begging, but also confirmed his begging by miracles, as appeared in the sailors, whom he sated with the remnants of his food and, having sated them, delivered; and he had it confirmed through the rule approved by the supreme Pontiff, in which he says[^p138-10] that «the brothers, in poverty and

<!-- page 139 -->

humility serving the Lord, may go for alms with confidence» etc.

24. Likewise, Gregory, in the *Morals*, on that text of Job[^p139-1]: *I have numbered to myself wearisome nights:* «The elect serve the Maker of things and are often straitened by want of things; through love they cleave to God, and yet they are in need of the supports of the present life». Therefore to be in need of the supports of the present life befits the perfection of the Saints.

25. Likewise, Jerome, in the forty-first epistle[^p139-2], to a certain hospitable man: «A great number of Saints is sustained by you throughout the islands of Dalmatia; but you would do better if you yourself too lived as a saint among the Saints». Therefore it is better to live on alms than to give alms.

Likewise, this same thing is shown *by reason.*

26. As the habit is, so also is the act[^p139-3]; but the act of one who is wholly poor, insofar as he is poor, is to be in need and to beg: therefore, if to be poor is good, to beg for Christ is praiseworthy and perfect.

27. Likewise, to beg is not prohibited by the law of nature, nor by the law of Scripture, nor by the law of grace; but what is not prohibited, being imposed through obedience, becomes praiseworthy and meritorious, above all if it is difficult and burdensome[^p139-4]: therefore, if some by profession have to beg, since they do this out of obedience, it will be praiseworthy and perfect for them.

28. Likewise, the Church imposes, as a penalty of satisfaction, begging for a time, or even perpetual begging; but it would not impose this unless it were satisfactory[^p139-5]; but he who voluntarily binds himself to a satisfactory work acts perfectly: therefore he who has vowed always to beg for Christ acts perfectly and praiseworthily in this.

29. Likewise, contempt of self belongs to perfection, and greater contempt to greater perfection; but he who begs for Christ most of all despises himself and casts himself down: therefore such a one acts most perfectly.

30. Likewise, voluntary poverty pleases God more than involuntary: therefore also begging proceeding from that poverty is more pleasing to God; but the first can be praiseworthy and meritorious: therefore much more the second.

31. Likewise, he who serves some lord merits to be sustained by him; but he who renounces all things and fulfils the counsels serves the greatest[^p139-6] Lord: therefore such a one most of all merits to be sustained by the Lord, therefore also by the servants of the Lord. If therefore one is voluntarily poor, by this very fact that he is such, if he requests alms, he requests justly.

32. Likewise, he who gives greater things can receive lesser; but the poor man, praying, bestows spiritual mercy: therefore much more strongly can he receive bodily alms.

33. Likewise, one preaching by authority can receive expenses by right of his power: therefore one preaching by sub-authority and commission or mandate can request them by supplication.

34. Likewise, he who serves another[^p139-7] is justly sustained by him; therefore those who serve the universal Church are justly sustained by the whole world: therefore, if there are any who, running through the world, serve the Christian people by word and example, they can deservedly request sustenance from it.

35. Likewise, if it is lawful to receive the greater, much more strongly is it lawful to receive the lesser; but it is lawful for poor religious, who have renounced their own things, to receive very ample possessions; therefore much more strongly is it lawful to receive daily sustenance. Otherwise it seems monstrous to say that it is lawful to receive a hundred marks of gold, and not lawful to receive a hundred pieces of bread. — *If you say* that it is lawful to *receive,* but not lawful to *request;* this is utterly nothing, because *to request* is ordered to *receiving* and exists for its sake; and just as *to give* is more than *to promise,* so *to receive* is more than *to request.* But it is certain that whatever it is lawful to give it is lawful to promise: therefore whatever it is lawful to receive it is lawful to request[^p139-8].

36. Likewise, if a friend requests a gift from his friend, he acts against no law, neither the one by requesting, nor the other by giving, nor the first again by receiving; but the law of charity and of divine love is more communicative than the social law; therefore if anyone requests that something be given him for the love of God, he offends in nothing and in no respect withdraws from perfection.

37. Likewise, it is undoubtedly lawful to request something in the giving of which the condition of the giver is made better; but he who gives something for God *lendeth to the Lord*[^p139-9] and exchanges a temporal thing for an eternal one and exercises himself in the work of the most noble virtue, namely piety. If therefore all these things befall him who gives by reason of the one who petitions, the poor man who requests alms acts most of all according to the law of charity; therefore he in no way withdraws from perfection.

<!-- page 140 -->

> **Conclusio.** *Begging that is done for the imitating of Christ, or for the evangelizing of Christ, or in both ways, is not to be blamed; rather it belongs to evangelical perfection.*

Rᴇsᴘᴏɴᴅᴇᴏ: For the understanding of what has been said it must be noted that begging comes about in three ways. And the first way is from the *necessity of nature;* and this way is when someone begs not only because he is poor, but also because he has *infirmity,* or *weakness,* or *both.* Now this way is wretched and tolerable, but through good patience it becomes meritorious and praiseworthy; as one reads in Luke sixteen[^p140-1] about Lazarus, that *there was a certain beggar named Lazarus, who lay at the gate of the rich man, full of sores;* and afterwards it is added that *the beggar died and was carried by the Angels into Abraham's bosom.* But of the rich man it is said that *he was buried in hell.* From which it is gathered that mendicancy, even when it comes from necessity, is an occasion that leads into the way of eternal salvation; according to what Gregory says[^p140-2], that the evils which press upon us here compel us to go to God.

The second way of begging is from the *viciousness of fault;* and this is when someone begs either for the *fostering of idleness,* or for the *heaping up of gain,* or *in both ways.* And this way, I say, is blameworthy in all who beg thus. Of whom Ambrose says in the book *On Duties*[^p140-3]: «For the sake of petitioning the able-bodied come, they come having no cause but that of wandering, and they wish to empty out the supports of the poor and to drain the store; nor content with a little, they seek greater things, catching at the favour of their petitioning by the display of their clothes, and by feigning a noble birth bidding up the increase of their gains». But although in all of them it deserves to be rebuked, it is nevertheless to be rebuked above all in those who have the appearance of sanctity. Of whom Augustine says in the book *On the Work of Monks*[^p140-4]: «It is in no way fitting that in that life, where senators become labourers, there workmen should become idlers; and that where they come, having left their own delights, they who were lords of estates, there peasants should become dainty». And afterwards, to rebuke their idleness and their gain-seeking, he adds[^p140-5]: «Yet the most cunning enemy has scattered everywhere many hypocrites under the habit of monks, going about the provinces, nowhere sent, nowhere fixed, nowhere standing, nowhere sitting; some hawk the limbs of martyrs, if indeed they are martyrs'; others *enlarge their fringes and their phylacteries;* others lie that they have heard their parents or kinsfolk are living in this or that region and that they are journeying to them; and all of them beg, all of them exact either the costs of a lucrative neediness, or the price of a feigned sanctity». — Against such men blessed Augustine inveighs much in the book *On the Work of Monks,* for this reason, that they not only fostered sloth in themselves by begging, but also persuaded others of it by preaching, ill understanding the Gospel[^p140-6], where he seems to suggest that men, like the birds, ought not to labour; and worse still, expounding the Apostle, where he urges the brethren to work, by saying that this is to be understood of spiritual, not of bodily labour. And because in this they were deceiving both themselves and others, therefore Augustine rebukes them and in many ways shows the contrary throughout the whole book *On the Work of Monks,* which is wholly against those who approve this second way of begging. Whence of such men he says[^p140-7]: «Men who were preaching sloth as though it were the preserver of the Gospel, accusing mercy as though it were the Gospel's betrayer. Would that these men, who wish to be at leisure with their hands[^tr-vacare-2], were wholly at leisure with their tongues too! For they would not invite so many to imitate them, if they set before them examples not only slothful but also dumb. But as it is, against the Apostle of Christ they recite the Gospel of Christ. For so marvellous are the works of the slothful, that they wish the Gospel to be hindered in the very thing which the Apostle therefore commanded and did, that the Gospel might not be hindered».

But the third way of begging is from the *supererogation of justice;* and this is when someone begs for the *imitating of Christ,* or for the *evangelizing of Christ,* or *in both ways.* — For *Christ,* I say, *to be imitated* as regards *contempt of self, affection for one's neighbour* and *the worship of God.* For in these three above all we ought to imitate Christ. And this indeed he does who voluntarily begs for the name of Christ, that he may make himself vile and humble himself, that he may build up his neighbour and stir him to piety, and that he may be free for God with a mind at liberty,

<!-- page 141 -->

having laid aside all care of the world. And of such men Augustine, in a certain Lenten sermon[^p141-1], says: «In this time, in so far as God commends the works of mercy, he has made his own Saints to be in need, so that, when they have been made the friends of God, they may *receive of the mammon of iniquity* and may themselves receive their friends *into the everlasting dwellings;* that is, so that, since the godly servants of God, while they are continually free for God, are sometimes indigent, those who have the riches of the world may lavish alms upon them; and as they make them partakers in earthly substance, so with them they may deserve to have a part in life eternal». — From which it appears that such begging is praiseworthy which is done for the *imitating of Christ*[^p141-2].

And likewise that which is done for the *evangelizing of Christ,* because it opens a way for the Gospel, by reason of *what* is preached, *by whom* it is preached and *to whom* it is preached. — By reason of *what is preached,* because we preach contempt of the world; which is more effectively urged by word and example than by word alone. — It is also *preached by more,* if those who preach live by the Gospel, than if they were compelled to live by their own means or labour. — It is also *more effectively received from more,* while those who receive the teaching lavish sustaining benefits. This is what John Chrysostom says on Matthew, in the thirty-second homily[^p141-3]: «It is manifest that the disciples had to be fed by those to whom they preached, so that neither should they themselves be high-minded against those who were being taught, as giving all things and receiving nothing from them; nor again should these be cut off, despised by them. Then, lest they should say, "You bid us live by begging", and be ashamed of it, he shows this to be a *debt,* and calls them *workmen,* and names what is given a *wage.* For do not, because the work consists in *words,* think the benefit that comes from you a *small* one. For it has many labours, and whatever those who are taught give, they do not bestow it *as men lavishing a gift,* but *as men rendering a recompense*».

When therefore anyone begs for the imitating and the evangelizing of Christ according to the form prescribed, not only does he not sin, but he even fulfils perfect justice: first, because sustenance is *much* owed to a just poor man *imitating Christ;* secondly, because it is *more* owed to him as to a Gospel poor man, *evangelizing Christ;* thirdly, because it is *most* owed to a grateful poor man, who humbly receives as a favour what is owed and humbly makes supplication, seeing that, being *free from all men, he makes himself the servant of all.* — And this is what Augustine says on that text of the Psalm[^p141-4]: *Who bringeth forth grass for cattle* etc.: «*Bringing forth,* he says, *grass,* that is, temporal things; *for cattle,* that is, for preachers, that *they who announce the Gospel may live by the Gospel;* and *herb,* that is, temporal things; *for the service of men,* that is, for preachers, the servants of men for Christ's sake. Whence the Apostle: *Whereas I was free from all men, I made myself the servant of all.* Which servitude is from charity, which makes men humble, who for Christ become the servants of men, intent upon the benefit of the brethren. These are the *cattle,* to whom *herb* is owed as a wage, because they serve. Of whom it is said: *Thou shalt not muzzle the mouth of the ox that treadeth out the corn;* and: *The labourer is worthy of his hire.* For temporal things are owed to preachers, because they lavish spiritual things. Whence the Apostle: *If we have sown unto you spiritual things, is it a great matter if we reap your carnal things?* For whom it is said: *Blessed* is he who forestalls the voice of one about to request, that is, *he that understandeth concerning the needy and the poor.* For you ought not to deal with the ox that treads out the corn as with a beggar passing by. For to him, that is, to the beggar, you give because it is read: *Give to every one that asketh;* but to this man you ought to give even *when he does not ask.* See therefore that, if the soldier of Christ is so indigent that he asks, he does not judge you, that is, show you to be damnable. For just as it is said of the man who seeks you out: *Give to every one that asketh;* so of that preacher, whom you ought to seek out, it is said: *Let thy alms sweat in thy hand, until thou find a just man to whom to give.* To every one that asketh, then, whoever he may be, give, discerning in him to whom you give; but much more give to the servant of God, the soldier of Christ, even when he does not ask». — From these things, therefore, it manifestly appears that in a preacher and evangelizer of Christ to beg is not a matter of vice or of fault, but rather of evangelical perfection. Whence the reasons and authorities and examples which are brought forward for this side are to be conceded[^p141-5].

For if anyone should wish to attack and disapprove every way of begging in the servants of Christ, he will seem to attack not only the order of the poor, but also the *supreme Pontiff* himself, who approved this way of living; and not only him, but also this *great company of Saints* who begged, namely Francis, Dominic, Ale-

<!-- page 142 -->

xius, Benedict and the very band of the Apostles and Prophets; and not only these, but also *the Lord Jesus Christ* himself, whom Scripture does not fear to call *poor* and *a beggar;* since our faith says and holds greater things of him, namely that for us he was not only *poor* and *a beggar* for our example, but also *stripped* and *made vile* even to the uttermost kind of abjection, that he might give an example of perfectly despising the world. — Lastly, over and above all these evils the *state of the universal Church* seems to be overthrown. For if it is unlawful for the poor of Christ to receive by the acceptance of alms the sustenance of life, much more strongly is it unlawful to receive the breadth and multitude of possessions and great revenues. For who is so absurd as to say that it is lawful for someone to receive a talent of gold, and not a piece of bread? And if this is true, then — since the possessions of all the churches, both among religious who have property and among secular clerics, are held through the acceptance of alms voluntarily and freely given — it will seem from this that the state of all the churches is overthrown, if to receive alms or to live on alms, whether in a small quantity or in a great, is judged unlawful; unless perhaps someone should say that it is lawful to *receive,* but not lawful to *request.* But this seems strange, that a man should be able to do what is greater, and not what is less[^p142-1]; and that some monastery should be able to receive a hundred thousand marks in revenues from some prince, and one poor little man not be able suppliantly to implore the relief of his indigence; and that someone should be able to request from a friend out of friendship, and a Christian not be able to request from a Christian out of charity; and that someone should be able to request certain things expedient for the security and abundance of some rich monastery, and a voluntary poor man not be able to request the sustenance of life; and that some scholar should be able lawfully to beg for the love of advancing in the knowledge of a learning that puffs up, so that he may at length come to temporal riches; and a poor religious not be able to request for the love of advancing in charity and humility, which builds up[^p142-2], so that he may at length come to eternal life — and other like things, which seem exceedingly absurd. If therefore all these are not small but great incongruities, it must be held that it is lawful for the poor to be sustained by the alms of others, at least according to the third way of begging explained above.

1. To that, therefore, which is objected in the contrary sense first, that *there is no want to them that fear God;* it must be said that want is twofold, namely *bodily* and *spiritual;* and that word is understood not of any sort of want, but of *spiritual* want. — And that this is true appears from what immediately follows[^p142-3]: *The rich have wanted and have suffered hunger;* it is certain that this is not understood of the need of material riches; it remains, therefore, that it be understood of the want of spiritual things, which, though it be in the rich, is nevertheless not in those who fear God; because *fear is the beginning of wisdom,* of which it is said in Wisdom seven: *Now all good things came to me together with her* etc.

2. To that which is objected from the Psalm: *I have not seen the just forsaken* etc.; it must be said that bread is twofold, namely *bodily* and *spiritual;* and that word is not understood of *bodily* bread, but of *spiritual* bread. And that this is true appears from the Gloss in the same place[^p142-4]: *Nor his seed seeking bread,* Gloss: «spiritual bread, that is, to be in need of the bread of the word of God, because the word of God is always with him». — The text can also be so construed that it is understood: *I have not seen the just forsaken, nor his seed seeking bread have I seen forsaken;* because, according to what is said on the last chapter to the Hebrews, on that text: *I will not leave thee, neither will I forsake thee;* Gloss: «He would be forsaken who should perish of hunger». The Prophet therefore means to say that he has seen neither the just man nor the just man's seed dead of hunger; and this does not call a man back from poverty, but rather invites him to it.

3. To that which is objected, that begging is an evil which the Psalmist, speaking in the Spirit of God, imprecates; it must be said that there is a *voluntary* begging and a *forced* begging, which is with murmuring and impatience. The first is meritorious, the second is demeritorious and afflicting; and the Psalm is understood of the second, not of the first. And that this is true appears from the text, because it is said[^p142-5]: *Let them be carried about vagabonds and be cast out;* and all these things seem to sound of compulsion and violence.

4. To that which is objected from Ecclesiasticus: *My son, in thy lifetime be not indigent;* it must be said that, according to the distinction already laid down, that word is to be understood of *spiritual* indigence in respect of grace, not of *bodily* indigence in respect of the sustaining of nature. And that this is true appears from the Gloss[^p142-6]: «*Be not indigent,* says the Gloss, of virtues or of good morals».

<!-- page 143 -->

5. To that which is objected, that it is a more blessed thing to give than to receive; it must be said that this is true when the acceptance of alms comes from the *necessity of nature;* but not when it comes from the *supererogation of justice,* according to the distinction made in the principal solution. — And that this is true appears from what is said in the book *On Ecclesiastical Dogmas*[^p143-1]: «It is good to distribute one's means to the poor with discretion, but it is better, with the intention of following the Lord, to give all at once and, released from care, to be in need with Christ». Augustine says this same thing in the book *On the Good of Marriage,* as was cited in the preceding question.

6. To that which is objected from the Gloss on Luke twelve[^p143-2], that when all things have been spurned for God's sake men ought to work with the labour of their hands; it must be said that this is a counsel as regards the *first* thing, which is said: *Sell what you possess;* but as regards the *second* it belongs only to the *well-being* of the counsel, which does not bind even perfect men, and least of all those who can be occupied about greater goods. — And that this is true appears from the same Gloss, when it is added: «whereby you may live, or may give alms». For it is certain that those who have once given all things for God are in no way bound to give further alms; and therefore that Gloss does not state what belongs to the *essence* and substance of the counsel, but rather what is according to its *well-being,* according to the state and condition of certain persons, who are more fit for working with their hands than for doing something more arduous. For if it were said otherwise, that this belonged to the *essence* of the perfection of the counsel, then no one would have fulfilled that counsel except those who worked with their hands; and by this we should judge that the Apostles other than Paul and Barnabas[^p143-3], and very many others of the most perfect Saints, whom we do not read to have worked with their hands, were not perfect. It is indeed true that manual work is in accord with evangelical perfection, provided that it does not hinder greater goods. Whence the Gloss on that text[^p143-4]: *I go a fishing:* «It must be known that they were not forbidden to seek their living by their own lawful craft, the integrity of the apostolate being preserved, when they had not whereby to live; and so, as they laboured, God added to them the necessaries which he had promised». — But that this labour belongs to those not occupied about greater things is manifest, because at that time the Apostles were by no means free for preaching. Whence Chrysostom[^p143-5]: «Simon came to fish, because Christ was not continually with them, nor had the Spirit been given, nor had anything been committed to them then, nor had they anything to do; and so they plied their craft». And therefore from this it does not follow that perfect men are bound to work with their hands, unless perhaps when they have no spiritual and better exercise. — Besides, grant that a perfect man were bound to work with his own hands; from this nevertheless it cannot be inferred that he may not, for his own humiliation and the building up of others, humbly request alms according to place and time.

7. To that which is objected from the Gloss on the first to the Thessalonians, chapter four, that nothing of another's is to be desired or requested; it must be said that a man can request something from another in two ways: either out of desire for *another's property,* or out of desire for *another's salvation.* The first the Apostle forbids as an evil and as forbidden in the Law, where it is said[^p143-6]: *Thou shalt not covet thy neighbour's goods;* but the second he does not forbid, because the law of charity grants it, which is nourished by mutual benefits and makes the burden of the one to be borne by the other. For what is so absurd as for us to believe that the Apostle denied that anyone should request from another either a benefit or help or the consolation of brotherly mercy and piety? — And that this is true appears from what the Apostle says in the last chapter to the Philippians[^p143-7] about himself, giving them thanks for the benefit conferred: *I seek not the gift, but I require the fruit abounding to your account.* For according to what Augustine expounds in the thirteenth book of the *Confessions,* he seeks the *gift* who takes his delight chiefly in the thing received; he requires the *fruit* who takes his delight in another's salvation. This is the sense of Augustine's words, although the words are changed.

8. To that which is objected from the Gloss on the first to the Thessalonians, chapter five, that *neediness makes a man turn aside from justice;* it must be said that, just as riches are not at fault, but the greed of riches; so neediness is not at fault, but impatience of neediness; and he who has this turns aside from justice, while, in fleeing neediness,

<!-- page 144 -->

he goes after money; and he who follows money easily turns aside from justice; *for they that will become rich fall into temptations and into the snare of the devil and into many desires hurtful and harmful*[^p144-1]. And just as such neediness is evil, so too is the begging that proceeds from such neediness to be blamed. — But when scarcity pleases a man more than abundance, and a man out of love of poverty despises the abundance of earthly things, such a one has no occasion of turning aside from justice on account of such neediness; and such is the poverty and the begging which belongs to evangelical perfection. — And that this answer is right is plain from the Gloss itself, in which it is said that «neediness, while *it desires to be filled,* turns aside from justice». For the needy man who *desires to be filled*[^p144-2] is the needy man who loves not poverty but satiety, not scarcity but abundance. And such a one is not the Gospel and heavenly poor man who begs from the supererogation of justice; but rather the greedy and earthly man who begs from the viciousness of fault, according to the answer given before.

9. To that which is objected from the Gloss on the second to the Thessalonians, chapter three, that the Apostle wills that the servants of God work bodily etc.; it must be said that this *willing* either is not the *willing of a precept,* but of an *admonition;* or, if it is the *willing of a precept,* it does not extend to all, but to those who wish to be in need; or, if it does extend to those who wish to be in need for Christ[^p144-3], it extends only to those who cannot do greater things. And from this it is not gathered that the life of the poor is evil who eat and do not work with their own hands, if they are occupied with better and more wholesome exercises. — And that this is true is manifest. For if that word: *He that will not work, let him not eat,* were a precept *without qualification,* then all would be in an evil state who do not seek their own food with their own hands, when they can; and this would be to condemn the whole world. — And that it does not extend to *voluntary and perfect poor men,* this appears from the Gloss itself, which says, «that they may not be compelled by neediness to request necessaries». For such men do not beg as those *compelled by necessity,* but as those *led on by the love of perfection.* — Lastly, that it does not extend save to those who are not occupied about greater things appears from the very text of the second to the Thessalonians, chapter three[^p144-4]: *We have heard that some among you walk disorderly, working not at all, but curiously meddling; now we charge them that are such, that working with silence they would eat their own bread.*

And that this is true is plain from Ambrose, who on that text of Luke nine[^p144-5]: *Let the dead bury their dead,* speaks thus: «The Lord teaches that lesser goods are to be passed over for the profit of greater. For it is greater to raise up by preaching the souls of the dead than to hide a dead body in the earth». Whence the Apostles too said, in Acts six: *It is not reason that we should leave the word of God and serve tables.* — It appears also from Jerome in the prologue on Job, who speaks thus: «If I were weaving a basket of rushes, or folding palm leaves, so as to eat my bread in the sweat of my face and to handle the belly's business with an anxious mind, no one would carp, no one would blame. But as it is, because according to the Saviour's word I wish to *labour for the meat which perisheth not*[^p144-6], and to clear of briars and brushwood the ancient path of the divine volumes; a twofold error is fastened upon me: as a corrector of the copyists I am called a *forger,* and one who does not take errors away but sows them». — This appears also from blessed Augustine in the book *On the Work of Monks*[^p144-7], where he says that those who «had in this world something by which they might easily sustain this life without handicraft, and who, being converted to the Lord, distributed it to the needy — such men are not bound or compelled to manual works, nor to seeking their own food». And afterwards, further down, he gives the reason: because those who have sustained the poor ought to be and can be sustained by the alms of the poor. For he speaks thus[^p144-8]: «There is one commonwealth of all Christians; and therefore whoever has distributed necessaries to Christians anywhere, from wheresoever he himself receives what is necessary for him, he receives it of the goods of Christ; because wherever he himself gave to such men, who received it but Christ»? — If therefore, according to Ambrose, greater goods are not to be passed over for the sake of lesser; and according to Jerome the goods of preaching and instruction are greater than those of manual work; and according to Augustine those who were rich and delicately bred are not bound to works of this kind — it manifestly appears that those intent on preaching and on the works of spiritual mercy are not bound to works of this kind, but ought to be and can be sustained by alms. And this is what Augustine in the same book[^p144-9], before these words, sets

<!-- page 145 -->

down: that men of this kind have such a power, if they are *evangelists, if they are ministers of the altar, if they are dispensers of the Sacraments.* For if by doing bodily works a man merits his own sustenance, much more strongly by bestowing spiritual works, which are deservedly preferred to bodily works.

I confess therefore what is true, that if anyone were so strong both in spirit and in body[^p145-1] that he could work bodily and nonetheless, according to place and time, be free for spiritual exercises and for the Church's benefit, he would in so doing act more perfectly. But who is fully fit for this, since one must sweat with much labour that the truth be known, that when known it be loved, and that when known and loved it be set before others? For this there is needed continuity of reading and of prayer and of meditation; and he who wishes to attend to these carefully can be little occupied about bodily labour. Bodily labour, therefore, is to be praised and urged, yet so that the fervour of prayer be not hindered, nor the fruit of preaching, nor regular observance.

10. To that which is objected from the Gloss, that one who comes to another's table must needs flatter; it must be said that the Gloss expounds itself in what it says: «given over to idleness»; in which it suggests that the man who frequents another's table and begs for the fostering of idleness is prone to cajolery and flattery. And what wonder, if he who loves idleness and pampers his belly breaks out into a flattering word? But from this the Gospel poor man is not condemned, who comes to another's table for the sake of evangelizing and imitating Christ. For if such men, by the very fact that they are poor and come to another's table, were *flatterers,* then, since Christ sent the Apostles to preach the Gospel and to live at the expense of another's table, he would rather have made his Apostles *flatterers* than true *preachers.* Christ himself too would have been the greatest of flatterers, who was frequently invited and ate in the houses of the Pharisees[^p145-2]. — Whence, if we understand rightly, the vice of flattery is attached rather to the worldly rich than to the Gospel and voluntary poor. Whence Chrysostom, in the ninetieth homily[^p145-3] on Matthew, speaking of the rich, says thus: «One must flatter many, both princes and subjects, and be indigent of many things and serve basely and dread and suspect and fear the eyes of those who suspect and fear the mouths of slanderers and of others and the desires of the covetous. But poverty is nothing of the kind, but wholly the contrary; it is a region not subject to thieves, but fortified, a quiet harbour and a wrestling-ground and a gymnasium of philosophy. Hear, whoever of these you are that are poor, and still more whoever of you desire to be enriched: it is not an evil *to be poor,* but *not to be willing to be poor;* and do not reckon poverty to be an evil, and it will not be an evil to you». And a little after: «Do not bring forward to me those who accuse poverty, but those who have shone through it. With this Elias, brought up in it, was caught away by that blessed rapture; with this Eliseus was illustrious, with this John, with this all the Apostles. But with avarice Achab, Jezabel, Giezi, Judas, Nero, Caiphas were condemned».

11. To that which is objected from the decree of Urban, it must be said that there is a threefold defect in the citing of that decree. The first, because that decree is addressed to bishops, who ought to feed those who beg, not to those who are in need. Whence just as that law of Deuteronomy fifteen[^p145-4]: *There shall be no one indigent and a beggar among you,* was not given against the mendicancy of the poor, but against the tight-fistedness of the rich — which is plain from what is added in the same chapter: *The poor will not be wanting in the land of thy habitation; therefore I command thee to open thy hand to thy needy and poor brother* — so this law too is given to bishops who have abundance, for the sustenance of poor clerics, and above all of those who are not in need voluntarily, but under compulsion, lest through indigence they be made undevout about the divine service. — The second defect is that the decree is brought forward to prove that clerics ought not to have property of their own, as appears at the end of the chapter[^p145-5], where it is said: «From all these things it is clear that it is in no way lawful for clerics to have property of their own; and if they have had it, they will not be clerics». Whence by that decree the way is not closed to the poor who wish to have nothing of their own, but rather opened. — The third defect is this: that just as having property in common has been confirmed and approved by the authority of the supreme Pontiff, namely Urban, as appears here; so the manner of living in the highest poverty has been approved by the authority of the supreme Pontiff, as is plain from the title *On the Excesses of Prelates: Nimis prava*[^p145-6] etc. Whence the decree of Pope Urban could not prejudice the authority of Innocent, Honorius and Gregory, who approved that manner both in the common law and in private privileges, because «an equal has no command over an equal[^p145-7]»; and a consti-

<!-- page 146 -->

tution is more firmly established the other way about. — Nor from this can instability or contradiction among the rulers of the Church be argued, because statutes can and ought to be changed according to place and time; as is plain in the statute on the degrees of kinship and in very many others, which were justly established at the beginning, and nonetheless others have been introduced[^p146-1] no less justly.

12. To that which is objected about the consecration of a church, it must be said that in the citing of that decree too there is a threefold defect: first, because that statute about the consecration of an unendowed church was made for the relief of bishops; secondly, for the spreading of divine worship; and thirdly, for the comfort of ministering clerics, who do not wish voluntarily to bind themselves to the vow of poverty. But the contrary of these is found in the case proposed. For both the bishops and the Church are less burdened by religious living on alms than if provision were made for them[^p146-2] in benefices and prebends; secondly, because the worship of God would not be increased but rather diminished if this manner of living were taken away; thirdly, because such voluntary poor men serve God more gladly by begging than they would even serve God for a great benefice, and are more content with tiny alms than they would be with great benefices and prebends.

13. To that which is objected about the law on able-bodied mendicants, it must be said that there is likewise a threefold defect in the citing of that law: the first, because that law was introduced against certain men who in a time of famine usurped the alms of the poor, feigning incapacity; the second, because such men were idle and brought no benefit to the commonwealth; the third, because such men, who were able to work and were then occupied about nothing[^p146-3] and wished to have abundance, were prone to evil deeds and to robberies. And therefore by a just judgement the ruler of the commonwealth kept such men from begging and punished them with a very heavy penalty. But it is wholly otherwise with poor little religious, in whom is found neither the feigning of incapacity nor the love of idleness nor the danger of robbery, and by this neither loss to the Church, but rather the love of perfect virtue; and therefore they are not kept away by human law, but rather are proclaimed and commended by the divine law.

14. To that which is objected, that to beg is indifferent; it must be said that to beg, so far as it is of itself, does not belong to perfection, unless the universal renunciation of all things is presupposed; which indeed is according to the divine counsel, in which the Lord declared to the young man that if he fulfilled that counsel, by that very fact he would come to the summit of perfection. For he says[^p146-4]: *If thou wilt be perfect, go and sell all;* nor did he add: hand it over to me, that I may put it in my purses and all things be in common; but rather: *give to the poor;* nor did he subjoin: go and earn your living by the labour of your hands; but rather: *come, follow me.* In which, according as the Gloss expounds, is understood a calling to contemplation, or to imitating and evangelizing Christ.

15. To that which is objected, that it belongs to perfection to serve freely and to flee obligations; it must be said that there is an obligation that comes from the *guilt of fault* and one that comes from the *affection of perfect charity.* The first obligation is to be avoided, but the second is to be accepted, according to what is said in Romans thirteen[^p146-5]: *Owe no man anything, but to love one another.* For the Lord praises the friendship of spiritual men which is made *of the mammon of iniquity;* and it belongs to the perfection of Christ's mystical body that the members should share with one another *in the matter of giving and receiving,* so that mutual indigence may be supplied in turn.

16. To that which is objected, that those who beg by profession do this either because they are poor or because they are preachers; it must be said that they do this because they are poor *imitators* and *evangelizers* of Christ. For they *imitate* Christ by the observance of regular discipline, in which they are free for the divine praises, for prayers, for vigils and for the other regular disciplines; and they worship God and attack the enemy and protect and defend what is weak and unfortified in the camp of the Church. — Again, because they *announce the word of truth;* and likewise they praise God and attack the enemy and build up and instruct their hearers. And therefore, since these works of theirs are very useful and highly necessary to the Church, no one doubts that they merit their own sustenance; and therefore they do not act unlawfully if they request it humbly and suppliantly. For just as the eye by seeing does work highly necessary to the hand, so that the hand cannot reproach the eye nor say: *I need not thy help*[^p146-6]; nay rather its work is useful to the whole body, and so it merits to be sustained, just as the hand and the foot and the other members which are exposed to labour: so also of these members of the Church it ought to and can be understood without absurdity, if anyone will look with a devout eye upon the work and the watchfulness of these eyes.

17. To that which is objected, that such men are imitators of Giezi, who minister spiritual things for temporal; to this the Gloss answers sufficiently enough

<!-- page 147 -->

on that text of the first to the Corinthians, chapter nine[^p147-1]: *A dispensation is committed to me:* «We ought not to evangelize in order to eat, but to eat in order to evangelize, so that food may not be the good that is sought, but the necessity that is added; that this may be fulfilled: *Seek ye first the kingdom of God and his justice, and all these things shall be added unto you*». Whence if anyone were to set his end in a temporal thing in preaching the word of God, without doubt he would be perverse, because he would make an end of the way, and he would be like Giezi and Balaam, who loved the wages of iniquity, and like the other false prophets, who *taught for hire and divined for gifts*[^p147-2]. But if they seek sustenance as a way ordered to the preaching of the Gospel, then they walk rightly and imitate the Apostles and in nothing depart from the path of truth. Whence on that text of Luke ten[^p147-3]: *Eating and drinking such things as they have; for the labourer is worthy of his hire;* the Gloss: «Note that for the one work of preachers two wages are owed: one on the way, which sustains us in our labour; the other in our homeland, which rewards us at the resurrection».

18. To that which is objected, that it belongs to the perfect to abstain from an evil appearance; it must be said that an appearance is evil when[^p147-4] a man can reasonably suspect evil, all the circumstances that occur being weighed; but if anyone rightly attends to these things, men preaching thus do not have the appearance of false preachers. For *false preachers* are so called by opposition to *true preachers.* And those are called *true preachers* whose preaching has *authority, truth* and *usefulness;* *false preachers,* therefore, are those who have the contraries of these, either without qualification or in part. — But if we are willing rightly to attend to the preachers of the modern orders[^p147-5], they have these three in themselves: *authority* indeed, because they are sent by the supreme Pontiff and by other prelates, who are the lords of the harvest; *truth,* as is plain, because they have preached faith and morals according to the law of the Gospel; *usefulness,* because they have called many from the state of fault to the state of grace or of penance: and therefore they have incomparably greater appearance and likeness, if anyone attends with a devout eye, to true preachers than to false ones; although in so great a number it can hardly be but that some, with Judas, love the purse[^p147-6] and stray from the straight road. For if the Lord did not grant this to the twelve Apostles, whom he personally chose, that they should universally be good; what wonder if he does not grant it to other congregations? Nor yet on this account are the good to be condemned with the evil, just as the evil are not just, although they are mingled with the good.

19. To that which is objected, that either they have authority or they do not; it must be said that to have authority is twofold: either from *first institution,* or from *commission* or mandate; or, in other words: either from *an office enjoined,* or from *a privilege granted.* The first is *principal* authority and belongs to power, and it pertains to the Apostles and to the seventy and their successors, who are bishops and other curates, as the Gloss says on Luke ten[^p147-7]. The second is a *less principal* authority and may without absurdity be called *sub-authority,* and it belongs to those who are sent by the Apostles as disciples, as Paul used to send his disciples to different places; and it belongs above all to him who is in the place of Peter, the chief of the Apostles, nay in the place of Christ, who is the universal Lord of the harvest, on account of which he is also said to have the fulness of power[^p147-8]. As therefore it belongs to the first, who have *principal* authority, to receive expenses *by right of their power,* as the Gloss on the second to Timothy, chapter two, says[^p147-9]: *The husbandman that laboureth must first partake of the fruits;* and there the Gloss: «To take necessaries for oneself from those among whom one serves God as a soldier, and whom one works as a vinedresser, or feeds as a flock, is not mendicancy, but power»: so to those to whom *sub-principal* authority belongs, it belongs to receive *by supplication.* Which the Holy Spirit ordained for the Church's benefit, that they might build up not only by the word of preaching, but also by the word and equally by the example of humility. He, therefore, who has learned from Christ *to be meek and humble of heart* — I marvel if he dares to condemn this humility and humiliation as reprobate.

20. To that which is objected, that it is safer and more perfect to follow men more approved and more wise; it must be said that, according to the disposition of divine wisdom, God disposes and orders all things in their own times. Whence, as in the first age of the Church he brought in men powerful both in miracles and in signs, such as

<!-- page 148 -->

were the Apostles and their disciples; and in the middle age men of understanding in the Scriptures and in living reasons[^p148-1]: so in the last age he has brought in men voluntarily begging and poor in worldly things. And this indeed was rightly fitting, that by the first idolatry and the portents of idols should be destroyed, by the second heresy, by the third avarice, which reigns most of all at the end of the world. — Nevertheless, because poverty is the foundation of evangelical perfection, and is itself as it were the completion of the same; therefore it flourished at the beginning of the Church, and it is fitting that it should flourish about the Church's final state.
