Whence, as Jerome resolves that question about the eating of flesh, distinction thirty-five, *Ab exordio*[^p148-2], saying that in the first age, before the flood, the eating of flesh was not in use, and afterwards, after the flood, it was commonly granted to all, and later, at the coming of Christ, abstinence from the eating of flesh was praiseworthy — whence he says in the same place that «the omega turns back to the alpha», that is, the final state agreed with the first — so it can fittingly enough be understood in the matter before us also. Yet as Jerome does not understand his words of abstinence from the eating of flesh as regards all, but only as regards monks; so neither do I think what is said of poverty is to be understood except as regards the *voluntary* poor, who have voluntarily renounced the world. — Yet I do not wish anyone to argue from this as though I were saying that *the day of the Lord is now at hand*[^p148-3], nor that I *make broad and enlarge* my own *fringes,* wishing to be justified before men; nor that I prefer the moderns to the ancient Saints, because in a great and lofty state many weak and imperfect men may be found; nor was this spirit of poverty lacking in times past. According to which Gregory says, in the third book of the Dialogues[^p148-4], of a certain most holy man, Isaac by name, who was a chief lover of poverty. Whence he says also of him: «When his disciples frequently suggested to him that he should accept, for the monastery's use, the possessions that were offered; he, a careful guardian of his own poverty, held firmly to this saying: a monk who seeks a possession on earth is not a monk. For he feared to lose the security of his poverty, as misers are wont to guard perishing riches. And so there his life shone out, by the spirit of prophecy and by great miracles, to all who dwelt far and wide». From which it appears that his teaching was not to be despised.

This also was shown above by manifold examples, and could be shown by many more still. And if there were no examples, the state of most excellent poverty is of itself praiseworthy enough, not only according to sacred Scripture, but also according to the teachings of the Saints, which commend the state of voluntary poverty as chief and perfect. As therefore it is very absurd to reprove the state of married rich men, and more absurd that of clerics and religious having possessions; so it also seems most absurd, if anyone be found who would reprove the state of the poor who serve Christ the Lord in the highest poverty and the most humble begging, according to the counsel set forth by the mouth of Jesus Christ and promulgated beyond reproach. Their cause Jerome defends in the letter which he wrote against Vigilantius[^p148-5]: «It has been reported to me, he says, that against the authority of the Apostle Paul, nay of Peter, John and James, who *gave to Paul and Barnabas the right hands of fellowship,* and had charged them *to be mindful of the poor,* you forbid any relief of expenses to be sent to Jerusalem for the use of the Saints. Doubtless, if I answer this, you will at once bark that I am pleading my own cause, you who have bestowed on all so great a bounty that, unless you had come to Jerusalem and spent or poured out your own money or your patrons', we should all be in peril of famine. I say this which the blessed Paul says and enjoins in almost all his letters, that in the churches of the Gentiles *on the first day of the week,* that is, on the Lord's day, all ought to contribute what is to be sent to Jerusalem for the relief of the Saints. Could he not have distributed in some other part of the world, and in those churches which, newly born, he was instructing in his own faith, what he had received from others? But he desired to give to the holy poor, who, leaving their little means for Christ, were converted with their whole mind to the Lord's service. For it would be long now, if I wished to go over all the testimonies from the rest of his letters, in which he does this and hastens with his whole mind that money may be sent by believers to Jerusalem and to the holy places, not for avarice, but for refreshment, not

<!-- page 149 -->

for the gathering of riches, but for the sustaining of the frail little body and the warding off of cold and hunger; this custom persisting in Judaea to this day, not only among us, but also among the Hebrews, that those who *meditate on the law of the Lord day and night* and have no portion in the land save God alone should be supported by the ministrations of the synagogues and of the whole world, but only out of equality, *not that others should have* refreshment *and others tribulation,* but that the *abundance* of some may *supply the want* of others». These things being seen, the answer to what has been said is plain enough.

### Rᴇᴘʟɪᴄᴀᴛɪᴏɴ ᴀɢᴀɪɴsᴛ ᴛʜᴇ ᴏʙᴊᴇᴄᴛɪᴏɴs ᴀꜰᴛᴇʀᴡᴀʀᴅs ᴍᴀᴅᴇ.

But to these things[^p149-1] which have been said someone has attempted to be adverse in manifold ways, wishing to refute some of them as *impertinent,* some as *false,* some as *inconsequent,* some as *doubtful,* but very many of them as *hostile to apostolic teaching.* And this he does in replying to a certain question collected less than sufficiently afterwards in the schools, saving the collector's grace, as may better appear in the question already named.

I. First, then, he reckons *impertinent* whatever we bring forward about the disciples of Christ sent to preach, because to them was given the *authority* necessary for receiving, not from *mendicancy,* but from *power.* But those who preach without authority are false prophets, and therefore in neither way, neither from *authority* nor from *mendicancy,* ought they to request. — And *if it be said* that such men are sent by the Pope and by the bishops; he says that they are to be fed by the bishops, according to what is said in the Extra, on the Office of the ordinary judge, *Inter cetera*[^p149-2]. A form, therefore, was given to those preaching by office and authority, but *for a time,* that impediments might be removed. And therefore the authorities both of the text and of the Glosses seem in this case impertinent to the matter proposed, according to him who seems to be adverse to the aforesaid question.

But it is easy to detect the sophistical appearance of this objection, if one understands the root of the power which is in the Apostles and in prelates and in the messengers sent by the Apostles. For it is certain that this was radically in Christ, according as the Apostle says to the Galatians, chapter one[^p149-3], that he was *an Apostle by Jesus Christ;* and John twenty: *As the Father hath sent me* etc. Now Christ, in sending the Apostles, gave them a twofold power, namely of *preaching* and of *exacting* their own *sustenance.* And it is evident that these powers are distinct; because, although a bishop can *once* in the year exact his procurations in one place, he can nevertheless preach whenever he will: therefore the supreme Pontiff, who holds the place of Christ on earth, can give both together, and can also separate the one from the other. Since therefore the supreme Pontiff and the prelates cannot exercise personally by themselves all the things that pertain to them, on account of the scattered peoples subject to them; the Holy Spirit ordained in the governance of the Church that he should sometimes send legates who should have both powers. And *again,* lest particular Churches should be burdened, he ordained that the bishops should send suitable persons to preach and to hear confessions, for whom they themselves should also provide, in the Extra, on the Office of the ordinary judge, *Inter cetera*[^p149-4]. He ordained also that he, with the prelates, should send voluntary poor men and skilled, to whom neither should the bishops be bound to furnish supplies, lest they be burdened by the multitude of expenses, nor should they have power to exact procurations, so as to take away the burden of their subjects; and thus men who should walk humbly as poor, content with little and taking only necessary things, and humbly making their need known, when there was need, to those who both could and would freely and without burden and with cheerfulness support and relieve them. Whence, as the decretal on the Office of the ordinary judge went forth from the Apostolic See, so also the confirmation of the order and rule of such poor men preaching the truth, in the Extra, on the Excesses of prelates, *Nimis prava,* as has been said above[^p149-5]. — Nor is this done to the prejudice, but rather to the help, of parish priests; nor is the Church prostituted by this, if God provides help for those who have the cure, so that they may guard the souls committed to them, of which they have the care, more perfectly uncorrupted from every corruption of sin, spiritual men assisting them, not as lords, or as having the cure, but rather as servants through the charity of Christ.

Such men, therefore, because being sent they preach in humility and poverty, are like apostolic men, not like false prophets. But because they do not have that fulness of authority which prelates have, and the Apostles had; therefore they are to be numbered among the *helps,* first to the Corinthians, chapter twelve[^p149-6]: *God hath set in the Church, first Apostles, secondly prophets, thirdly doctors, after that miracles, then helps* etc.; the Gloss: «*Helps,* that is, those who

<!-- page 150 -->

bring resources to their greater ones, as Titus to the Apostle, and archdeacons to bishops». Among these also can be numbered those whom the Shepherd of the Church of God has appointed to bring help to others. And therefore I said, according to the usage of theological speech — which says that *sub-authority* is in him who is sent — that they have sub-authority, not *to make excuses in sins*[^p150-1], as is laid to my charge, but rather for the understanding of the truth. The form, therefore, prescribed to the Apostles both ought to and can belong to these men.

For as to what is said, that it was *temporary;* since it has no authority from the Scriptures, it is despised with the same ease with which it is proved. — *Again,* if the form was fitting in the primitive Church, why not likewise in the final one, chiefly as regards those who wish to imitate the Apostles in this form? For I do not say that the universal Church is in all things to be reduced to poverty — as is laid to my charge by them — as appears from my words above[^p150-2]; but those who wish can imitate the perfection of the Apostles and the height of poverty. — Whence by the example of the Apostles, from the words of the Gospel recited in the Church, blessed Francis was stirred up to take up this manner of life, as shines out more clearly from his legend[^p150-3]. — Since therefore an order of this kind is founded upon those words, they are not *impertinent,* but in every way pertinent, as the most stable foundations of living in this way. Such men, therefore, are to be received, because they set out as poor for the name of Christ, as is said in the Canonical letter of John[^p150-4], that those who receive such are *fellow-helpers of the truth.* Therefore those who are adverse to such men and do not receive them, or say they are not to be received, are like Diotrephes, of whom the Gloss says that he was also a heresiarch of that time; whose teaching and sect be far from all Christians.

II. He attempts to refute some things as *false.* — For he asserts it to be false that Christ requested anything as a beggar. For even if he is called *a beggar* in the Psalms, this is said inasmuch as he made request of the Father, or inasmuch as he took upon himself our infirmities. — If it be said that he requested water from the Samaritan woman and lodging from Zacchaeus[^p150-5], this was not from *mendicancy,* but from *authority,* or from *courtesy.* — And *again,* even if he had begged, it would not be *to be drawn into a precedent.* — He says it to be false nonetheless that any begging is *voluntary;* nay, that every begging must be unwilling and forced.

But it is easy to convict this objection, which fastens falsity upon truth, of falsity, if anyone will devoutly hear the truth. — For that Christ *was a beggar,* both the prophetic text and the Glosses testify; and that he *requested as a beggar,* as was alleged in the objecting[^p150-6] in two Glosses; and likewise in that Psalm: *O God, come to my assistance,* on that text: *But I am needy and poor;* the Gloss says: «He takes to himself the words of God, who in the preceding Psalm said these same things of himself, as if he said: They *who seek my soul* are rich; but I am *needy,* that is, one who requests, and *poor,* that is, insufficient for myself: because he neither has worldly abundance, and within, where he is rich, he always reaches out, desires and receives». According to this it is evident that Christ *was needy and poor* and *one who requests as one needy,* not from the Father only; for then all would in this way be beggars, and none rich, even those having abundance; which is against the Gloss. *Moreover,* Christ still makes request of the Father[^p150-7], the Angels make request, and the saints who see him face to face; and yet they are not called *beggars.*

Since therefore the text says this by so express a testimony, and the Gloss agrees, and no authority contradicts; why shall we not confess that Christ requested as a beggar? For we confess that he was a virgin and lacked a wife, although Scripture makes no mention of this. — The *Gospel* also expresses that he requested both drink and lodging; and it is evident that it was not from command and lordship and *authority,* because then he would not have given an example of poverty. — He would also have said what was false, that he had not where to lay his head[^p150-8], since he had subjects from whom he could exact procurations; and he would have acted less justly in this, that he preached in Jerusalem and was fed in Bethany, working fruit and preaching in one place, and receiving the emolument in another.

But you will say that he requested from *courtesy.* But did Christ take poverty upon himself to teach courtesy? Was it for the teaching of human civilities that he willed to become needy and poor? Does he call himself

<!-- page 151 -->

a master of courtesy and not rather of humility? *Learn of me,* he says, Matthew eleven[^p151-1], *because I am meek and humble of heart.* He requested, therefore, from *humility* what was opportune for relieving his own indigence, and by this as a poor beggar.

And it is evident that he did not have this indigence except because he willed it; nor did he request, except because he willed: because he could turn stones into gold when he willed; as his disciple John the evangelist did[^p151-2] for those men who were unwilling to beg and to be in need and were evangelizing, who had turned back, and who afterwards preferred mendicancy and poverty to precious gems. — And by this it is evident that it belongs to Christ and to those who follow him to beg *voluntarily;* otherwise they would in no way merit in this begging and poverty. For it is absurd to say that poverty and neediness are voluntary, and that the begging that proceeds from them is forced.

If, therefore, begging in Christ was from poverty and humility and voluntary; why is it not *to be drawn into a precedent?* since he himself says: *Learn of me, because I am meek and humble of heart;* since he also invites the rest who wish to come to the highest summit of perfection to follow him through poverty of this kind: *Go,* he says[^p151-3], *and sell* etc.; and the Apostle says, second to the Corinthians, chapter eight: *You know the grace of our Lord Jesus Christ,* and afterwards: *I give counsel,* the Gloss: «that you may imitate his poverty»; since this also is that perfection which is set forth to all in common, and it is said[^p151-4]: *He that can take, let him take;* since Augustine on John says, in the fifteenth homily: «In all things which Christ did as man, he was furnishing an example to men who would believe in him»; since Jerome says that even if he is not to be imitated by us in miracles, in those things which he did humbly he is to be imitated by us; since he was also the universal form of the virtues in his acts. Whence Augustine, On True Religion[^p151-5]: «No sin can be committed except while those things are sought which he despised, or those things are fled which he endured. And so his whole life on earth, through the man whom he deigned to take, was a discipline of morals». — Who, therefore, would dare to say that it is not *to be drawn into a precedent,* except one who dares to say that imitation is not owed to Christ in those things which he did humbly? But if we say this, whatever there is of sanctity, virtue and perfection, and the Christian religion, is confounded and subverted[^p151-6], and is also enfeebled, because by the same reasoning by which this is said, by the same reasoning it will be possible to say it of the other examples of the virtues also. But far be this from all Christian hearts.

III. He strives nonetheless to refute some things as *inconsequent.* — For he says that it does not follow that, although *neediness* is suitable to the perfection of the Saints, on that account *mendicancy* is too, which sometimes accompanies neediness; which might likewise be argued of the eating of one's children and of thefts and robberies, which often follow upon neediness and poverty. — He says it also to be inconsequent that, although blessed Benedict was perfect, on that account, in receiving daily alms from that Roman[^p151-7], he did a work pertaining to perfection, because a perfect man does many things which do not pertain to perfection, such as eating, evacuating and other like things. — He says it also to be inconsequent that, although *to make oneself vile* and *to humble oneself* for Christ is good, on that account *to humble oneself by begging* is good, because *to beg* is not *to humble oneself,* but to take care of oneself. And *again,* a harlot makes herself vile by prostituting herself, and yet in this she is not pleasing to the Lord; in like manner neither is the making vile by which someone makes himself vile by begging.

But these foul instances, since he sets forth foul examples and strives to befoul mendicancy, it is easy for a spiritual man[^p151-8] to spit out as foul, false and feigned. For we find that Christian and perfect men are not praised in their legends for cruelties in eating men, or for cupidities in taking plunder, or for evacuations, fornications and the like; but they are found to be praised and proclaimed for this, that they chose to live in so great a scarcity that they had neither food nor clothing, nor did they wish to have them as the rich do, but rather to beg humbly as poor and despised men, as appears in the examples introduced above[^p151-9]. — For that the eating of one's children should follow upon neediness, this is against the *order of nature;* that theft and robbery should follow, this is against the *order of morals and of justice.* — But that there should follow the making known of one's necessity to a neighbour and the request

<!-- page 152 -->

for help for the love of God, this is according to the *order of charity and piety,* both natural and also gratuitous and spiritual. For every law commands that neighbour be cherished and relieved by neighbour. How, therefore, is that which perverts the order and is according to a perverse order like that which is done according to a right, just and good order, charitable and pious? — The Roman monk, therefore, is praised, because according to the law of charity he provided for blessed Benedict, and Benedict, who most humbly received[^p152-1] Nowhere, however, will you find him praised because he evacuated and voided; for it is exceedingly abominable to bring this forward when the praises of the Saints are being treated.

Now the beggar is praised, although *he takes care of himself,* not because he does this *in any way whatever,* but because he does it *most humbly;* whence, as *to eat soberly* is an act of virtue, although *to eat* is to take care of oneself; so it is to be understood in the matter before us. Whence Chrysostom, in the fourth homily[^p152-2] on Matthew, speaking of the holy youths who were in the palace of the king of Babylon, says thus: «Being therefore holy and just, they would ten thousand times have chosen to beg at home rather than to enjoy the goods that were in the temple. For *I have chosen,* he says, *to be an abject in the house of my God, rather than to dwell in the tabernacles of sinners.* And so ten thousand times they would have chosen to be abjects at home rather than to reign in Babylonia». There is, therefore, in begging a humbling and a making of oneself vile that is acceptable to God, otherwise it would not have been chosen by the holy youths; and it is *chosen* because it is care of oneself together with one's humility. But those who take *care of their flesh in its desires and concupiscences*[^p152-3], they without doubt are not praised, even if they seem to humble themselves and make themselves vile, like the harlot who prostituted herself for the love of money or for the heat of lust. But far be this from the hearts of the faithful, that the humbling of spiritual men in begging should be like the making vile of a harlot in her prostitution. For *the members of Christ are not to be taken away, that they may be made the members of a harlot*[^p152-4]. But Christ, of himself in the form of a servant and also for his members, says: *I am a beggar and poor, but the Lord is careful for me.* Which he would in no way say, unless *to be a beggar* for Christ so pleased God as to make God bear a most special care of us, as a father bears of his sons; and to think this of harlots and of those who play the harlot be far from Christian ears and minds.

IV. He attempts nonetheless to refute certain other things as *doubtful.* — For to that which is said of blessed Alexius, that he begged, and of blessed Francis, that he confirmed this by miracles; he answers: *Prove it,* as to things[^p152-5] doubtful and uncertain; adding that even if miracles had been done, it would not yet be certain: because many evil men work miracles, and because miracles could be done even with loaves taken away by stealth; nor yet would it be established from this that it was good to steal. — He says it also to be doubtful that the Roman Church approved begging: because, if she did this, she would be convicted of erring; likewise also, if she imposed it as a penalty of satisfaction, she would err; and this he says is by no means to be believed.

But this objection, which shelters itself behind a doubt, must without doubt be resisted. For if that which is read in the legend of saint Alexius and in the legend of blessed Francis be called into doubt; by parity of reasoning whatever is read in the legends of the other Saints comes into doubt: therefore all the examples of the virtues and the deeds of the Saints are now called[^p152-6] into doubt. But it is dangerous to imitate or believe doubtful things; and by this the faith, devotion and reverence which is had toward the Saints perishes, if the other things which are told of them be called into doubt. — *Again,* if the miracles which they did are not efficacious testimonies for establishing their sanctity, the Church plays the fool today, which on account of the testimonies of miracles canonizes Saints and ascribes them to the catalogue of the Saints. — *Finally,* who has ever heard that God multiplied for the salvation of others a loaf taken by a thief through stealth? Yet we have heard that through a poor beggar a loaf, which he had got by begging, was multiplied manifold[^p152-7]. — *But if you say* that this was a special privilege, and was done by the familiar counsel of the Holy Spirit; how did he hand this down to others as a law and rule, in which he invites them to begging, if it was inspired to him alone for himself alone? — *If you say* that it was[^p152-8] out of his own head, not by inspiration; how can he not be damned who rashly invited men to a reprehensible act as to an act of morals and of perfection? How, too, did the Roman See approve this with the consent of the cardinals?

*But if you say* that this is not to be believed, that she imposes mendicancy on anyone as a *penalty,* or that she approves it as a *rule* and a holy life; yet[^p152-9] that she imposes the mendicancy of pilgrimage is had in the treatise on Penance[^p152-10], where it is said of a priest who reveals confes-

<!-- page 153 -->

sions, «that he be deposed and go on pilgrimage in ignominy all the time of his life». But if he were to live by honest labour, or by his own expenses, he would not go as one vile, but rather as one honourable. It remains, therefore, that what she imposes is that he go *as a beggar.*

And that she approves it as a *rule* is plain from that writing alleged above[^p153-1], on the Excesses of prelates, *Nimis prava,* where it is said that «the Apostolic See is known to have approved the order and rule of the friars Preachers and Minors». It is plain also from the rule of blessed Francis, approved and confirmed by the lord Honorius, where it is said that the brothers «should go for alms confidently». But if she erred in approving this — and it is evident that she approved and confirmed it; it is evident also that the universal Church throughout the whole world accepts orders and states of this kind — the conclusion must then be, according to the saying of this one man, that the whole universal Church erred and was deceived; and that those who found out and approved this erroneous state are all damned; which is most horrible and most incredible, that God should permit his holy people thus[^p153-2] to err universally, and so great a multitude of wise men who went before these times. But who is this man, so *wise* and so *just,* that he dares to judge all the rest besides himself to have been deceived and reprobate? Marvellous is this *wisdom,* which shows the folly of all; and marvellous the *justice,* which condemns the rest. But if this is not wisdom, but rather rashness, to wish to judge the Apostolic See, which is judged by God alone, and to reprove its judgement and sentence[^p153-3] and to thrust so many holy men into hell and into the pit of damnation; let these things, therefore, be far from faithful and Christian hearts.

V. He attempts also to refute very many things, nay almost all, as *hostile to apostolic teaching,* on the ground that he says begging was forbidden by the Apostle as evil and was granted only in a particular case. — He says also that I have lied and have defended and do defend what is false against Scripture and the Apostle, in this that he says that I said that to labour with manual work is not a precept. He says therefore that *to beg* is not indifferent, but evil; and therefore that by the addition of a circumstance it can in no way become good; nay, what is more, in preachers he asserts it to be simoniacal.

But it is easy to observe that this foundation of his position, repeated rather frequently, is ruinous together with everything that is built upon it. For if *to beg* is forbidden, and *to beg* is to sin: therefore *to be a beggar* is *to be a sinner.* Since therefore Christ calls himself *a beggar,* nay the Prophet[^p153-4] does so in the person of Christ; Christ therefore confesses that he is a sinner; which to say or to believe is no small outrage or sin. — *Again,* if *to beg* is to sin, mendicancy or begging is a sin. But second to the Corinthians, chapter eight[^p153-5], on that text: *That by his poverty you might be rich;* the Gloss says: «Let no one despise himself: the poor man in his cell, rich in conscience, sleeps more securely on the ground than the man rich in gold in his purple. Do not, therefore, be afraid to approach with your mendicancy him who is clothed with our poverty; where he made himself poor, he enriched us». Therefore mendicancy disposes, and does not draw back, from the divine approach; it is evident, therefore, that it is not a sin.

Further, Chrysostom on Matthew, in the sixty-ninth homily at the end[^p153-6], says that «to beg in no way offends God; but to steal or to seize offends God and man». This is plain also from the authority of Chrysostom alleged above from the fourth homily on Matthew. It is not, therefore, forbidden by the Apostle, nor is it found anywhere that the Apostle forbade this, but rather that he commended it together with his fellow-apostles. For in the first to the Corinthians, chapter sixteen[^p153-7], he both preached and procured that collections should be made for the poor of the Saints who were in Jerusalem, as alms freely bestowed on them. And to the Galatians, chapter two, he says that *Peter and James gave to me and to Barnabas the right hands of fellowship, that we should go unto the Gentiles, and they unto the circumcision, only that we should be mindful of the poor;* the Gloss: «*Of the poor,* who had laid the price of their sold goods at the feet of the Apostles, *we should be mindful,* by making collections for their work». If, therefore, the Apostle was not simoniacal in this, that he requested collections and sent disciples for this, that they should request alms from those among whom he had sown spiritual things, for the poor who were in Jerusalem; if the supreme Pontiff should establish poor Orders, who should sow spiritual things and live on the free alms, requested or freely offered, of those whom they build up by word and example: he in nothing at all disagrees with the Apostle, nay rather he agrees, since he has as

<!-- page 154 -->

great power as he, and greater, in governing and disposing the members of the Church itself.

But *you will object* that the Apostle enjoined bodily labour in the text, and that the Glosses[^p154-1] seem to say that this tells against mendicancy.

But to this there is a plain answer: granted that the Apostle enjoins manual labour of this kind — which I have never denied, as appears in my words, but that he does not enjoin it *universally* on the servants of Christ, except perhaps in a case of necessity, in which no lawful way of having food lies open save by the labour of the hands — yet he enjoins it on *determinate* persons, according as appears from the text[^p154-2]: *We have heard there are some among you who walk disorderly, working not at all, but curiously meddling. Now we charge them that are such, and beseech them by the Lord Jesus Christ, that working with silence they would eat their own bread.* And a little before: *We charge you that you withdraw yourselves* etc.; the Gloss: «Here he begins about the curious and the idle, admonishing that they be corrected». It is plain, therefore, from the sequence of the text and from the Gloss, that that command extends to the curious and the idle. — Likewise, first to the Thessalonians, chapter four[^p154-3]: *We entreat you, brethren, that you use your endeavour to be quiet, and that you work with your own hands, as we commanded you;* the Gloss: «*Work,* which helps you to be quiet. For that evil comes of idleness». From this also it is plain that he says this for the avoiding of idleness and the removing of sloth, which is the bilge of desires and the fomenting of sins. Whence to the Ephesians, chapter four[^p154-4]: *He that stole, let him now steal no more; but rather let him labour, working with his hands* etc. — From this, therefore, it is plain that labour is not enjoined on *all;* and by this begging is not prohibited, save that which comes from idleness and cupidity, according as the text and the Glosses suggest.

But *you will object* concerning Augustine, On the Work of Monks, who was speaking to monks who were occupied and intent upon spiritual exercises, not idle; and to them he says that they are bound to works of this kind.

To this a manifest answer appears, if one attends to the conclusion near the end, by which he confutes the position of those who repudiated bodily work; in which they thought ill, there[^p154-5]: «Wherefore those who, having left behind either ample or any sort of wealth, have wished to live among the poor of Christ, if they are strong in body and are free from ecclesiastical occupations[^tr-vacare-3]; yet if they too work with their hands, they act more mercifully than when they divided all their goods among the needy. But if they will not, who would dare to compel them? But those who, outside that holy fellowship, were passing their life by bodily labour, if they do not labour, let them not eat».

It appears, therefore, who they are that are bound, namely the able and strong, and those who are free from ecclesiastical occupations, and those who were passing their life in the world by bodily labour, and many such there were among those monks, and there were more[^p154-6] at the beginning of the religious order of the friars Minor, and therefore they had to be constrained to labours; but now in comparison with the others very few are such: and therefore it is not against the Apostle if to such men it is granted simply[^p154-7] to beg, especially since such an act is not much to be craved nor delightful nor honourable, but rather filled with many vilenesses and miseries, to which the heart of no able man could be inclined save in the hope of the eternal reward and the love of Christ and the desire of profiting, with the help of the grace of the Holy Spirit, without which I scarcely believe a rich man could be inclined to the discomfort of so great an abjection. Nor do such men burden the people of the Lord and the Church, especially if they request nothing beyond what is necessary, because they would spend and consume far more of the alms of the poor if they had remained in the world as clerics in prebendal benefices; nor then[^p154-8], when they would be little intent upon spiritual works, would the way of salvation be closed to them even in riches. Far be it, therefore, that it should now be closed in so great a straitness of poverty.

From what has now been said in this question on poverty, that we may gather up the rest in sum, it appears that, as *to beg* in the poor who are weak and infirm is a *necessity of nature,* and in the able who are idle and covetous is a *viciousness of fault;* so in the voluntary poor, who despise themselves and imitate Christ and preach the Gospel, it is an *act in harmony with,* not repugnant to, *evangelical perfection,* on the ground that to *renounce* all things universally, appropriating no thing to oneself, is *counselled*[^p154-9] by the Lord as perfect; to *receive* alms is *granted* to the just poor as lawful and in nothing hostile to evangelical perfection; to *request* alms *humbly* is *not prohibited* to the voluntary poor as evil; to pass one's life *by the gain of one's own labour* is not enjoined on all the able poor as a precept binding of necessity.

<!-- page 155 -->

If anyone, therefore, impugns the *universal renunciation* of temporal things, he impugns Christ poor and crucified; he impugns the evangelical counsel; he impugns the company of the Apostles saying[^p155-1]: *Behold we have left all things;* he impugns also the Holy Spirit, who inspires and suggests this to the hearts of the perfect; he impugns God the Father of all things himself, who is the *refuge of the poor;* he impugns finally the kingdom of heaven, the whole universe, the lordship of which is granted to the poor man, the Lord saying: *Blessed are the poor in spirit, for theirs is the kingdom of heaven;* and therefore against such a *senseless* man it is necessary that *the whole world fight.*

But he who impugns the *receiving of alms* impugns Christ also, who received a stipend from the women who served him and invited him, Luke seven[^p155-2]; and the company of the Apostles, who also themselves received alms, which *were laid at their feet,* Acts four; and the state of monks, who are founded upon alms; and the whole clergy — for they too live on alms, which have been freely bestowed on the churches of Christ — and consequently the common state of the poor, which nevertheless the divine Scriptures declare to be most acceptable to God.

But he who impugns the humble *request for alms* in those who are in need and can lawfully receive, impugns *Christ,* who humbly requested lodging, who also confesses that he[^p155-3] is a beggar; he impugns also *Paul,* who requested that collections be made for the holy poor, and this with Peter's consent; he impugns the *bond of charity,* which makes men communicate *in the account of giving and receiving;* he impugns the *office of humanity,* through which one in need sets forth his necessity, lest he be inhuman to himself, and stirs up another, who can and will, to be human and pious to his neighbour; and in this no one is offended, no one is prejudiced, nothing is inordinately coveted, nothing is unduly taken away, especially in those who seek not the *gift,* but the *fruit*[^p155-4].

But he who impugns *idleness, cupidity, carnality and superfluity* in other Christians and especially in religious, does well and rightly, provided that he proceed according to a right order.

But he who universally lays upon all the able the *burden of gainful labour,* by which they ought to be sustained, enlarges the Apostle's precept too far and weighs down too heavily the yoke of servitude upon the servants of Christ, and holds back spiritual men from spiritual occupations and labours, to the peril of the salvation of souls, while he makes them more solicitous about feeding their own dying bodies than about gaining their neighbours' souls for the Lord. And if this be done, *mint and cummin are tithed, and the weightier things of the Law are left, judgement and mercy*[^p155-5]; bodily mercy is preferred to spiritual; and by this the body is preferred to the spirit, private advantage to the common good, which consists especially in the building up of the people of Christ, in alms, in teachings and counsels of souls, for which God has sent these last orders through the supreme Pontiff and the prelates. And if anyone should say that for these things they are *not sent,* he manifestly runs counter to *the truth,* since express testimonies to this are had both in the rule and in the privilege and institution and approbation of this twofold order[^p155-6]. — But if he should say that they *ought not to be sent,* he detracts from *evangelical perfection.* For who is more fit for this than he who has vowed evangelical perfection? — If anyone should say that they *cannot be sent* by the Pope and the bishops, he derogates from *apostolic authority* and from the *power of the keys* in prelates and from the *fulness of power* in the supreme Pontiff, who on earth, as[^p155-7] Scripture asserts, faith holds, the laws bear witness, and irrefragable reasons also convince, as the one and highest head and the only bridegroom and the chief hierarch, in whom is the state of the whole Church militant, holds the place of Christ. And therefore every knee ought to be bowed to him, of princes and of prelates and of clerics and of laymen and of religious on earth, after the likeness of Christ in heaven, to whom *every knee is bowed of those that are in heaven, on earth and under the earth*[^p155-8]. Amen.

---

## Apparatus

> The numbered footnotes below correspond to markers in both the Latin body above and the English translation. Quaracchi restarts the numbering on each printed page; the labels here carry the page.

[^p134-1]: **La.** In hac quaestione usi sumus codd. A D E K M. Eadem invenitur etiam in cod. L, sed in forma valde diversa, quia, ut dicitur in fine eiusdem, est a quodam clerico *reportata,* et quidem «minus sufficienter». Nam ad eandem respexisse videtur S. Doctor in principio *replicationis* ad obiectiones denuo factas, quae immediate post hanc quaestionem sequitur. Verba ipsius sunt: «respondendo cuidam quaestioni *minus sufficienter* post collectae in scholis, salva gratia collectoris». Nihilominus speciminis gratia posuimus in notis plura argumenta pro utraque parte, sicut exhibentur (in fundam. plerumque valde *insufficienter*) in codice L. Plura de hoc dicuntur in Prolegomenis nostris.

     **En.** In this question we have used the codices A D E K M. The same is found also in codex L, but in a very different form, because, as is said at its end, it was *reported* by a certain cleric, and indeed «less than sufficiently». For the holy Doctor seems to have had regard to it at the beginning of the *replication* to the objections made anew, which follows immediately after this question. His words are: «in replying to a certain question collected *less than sufficiently* afterwards in the schools, saving the collector's grace». Nevertheless, by way of specimen, we have set down in the notes several arguments for each side, as they are given in codex L (in the fundamenta, mostly very *insufficiently*). More is said about this in our Prolegomena.

[^p134-2]: **La.** Psalm. 33, 10. — Post *timentibus eum* L addit: *sed omnis mendicus est inops: ergo nullus mendicus est timens Deum; sed timor Dei est de perfectione religionis christianae: ergo etc.*

     **En.** Ps. 33:10. — After *timentibus eum* ("to them that fear him") L adds: *but every beggar is in want: therefore no beggar is one who fears God; but the fear of God belongs to the perfection of the Christian religion: therefore etc.*

[^p134-3]: **La.** Psalm. 36, 25. — L arg. complet addens post *panem* haec: *sed omnis mendicus quaerit panem: ergo nullus mendicus iustus; sed miseria est(!) de perfectione christiana: ergo mendicare non est de perfectione eiusdem.*

     **En.** Ps. 36:25. — L completes the argument, adding after *panem* ("bread") these words: *but every beggar seeks bread: therefore no beggar is just; but misery is(!) of Christian perfection: therefore to beg is not of the perfection of the same.*

[^p134-4]: **La.** Psalm. 108, 10: Nutantes... mendicent et eiiciantur de habitationibus suis. — L prosequitur: *hic Propheta loquitur per Spiritum sanctum: ergo Spiritus sanctus maledicit mendicantes, et ita mendicare non est opus perfectionis christianae.*

     **En.** Ps. 108:10: Vagabonds... beg; and let them be cast out of their dwellings. — L continues: *here the Prophet speaks through the Holy Spirit: therefore the Holy Spirit curses mendicants, and so to beg is not a work of Christian perfection.*

[^p134-5]: **La.** Vers. 29: Fili, in tempore vitae tuae ne... indigere. — L subiungit: *sed constat, quod loquitur Salomon per Spiritum sanctum: ergo Spiritus sanctus inhibet mendicantis* (mendicantibus), *ergo etc.*

     **En.** Verse 29: My son, in the time of thy life be not... to be indigent. — L subjoins: *but it is certain that Solomon speaks through the Holy Spirit: therefore the Holy Spirit forbids mendicantis* (mendicantibus, "to mendicants"), *therefore etc.*

[^p134-6]: **La.** Vers. 35: Beatius... accipere. — L prosequitur: *et ita dare est perfectionis: ergo accipere, eius oppositum, non est perfectionis; sed mendicare est accipere vel velle accipere: ergo mendicare non est perfectionis, immo potius imperfectionis.* — In conclusione ex D E posuimus *multo minus* pro *multo magis.*

     **En.** Verse 35: It is a more blessed thing... to receive. — L continues: *and so to give belongs to perfection: therefore to receive, its opposite, does not belong to perfection; but to beg is to receive or to wish to receive: therefore to beg does not belong to perfection, but rather to imperfection.* — In the conclusion we have put, from D E, *multo minus* ("much less") for *multo magis* ("much more").

[^p134-7]: **La.** Vers. 33. — Glossa, quae est *ordinaria* apud Strabum et Lyranum, est secundum Bedam (in hunc loc.): Ut, omnibus vestris [ita etiam L, qui ordinem argg. hic invertit] semel pro Domino spretis etc. — Post verba Glossae prosequitur L: *perfectionis igitur christianae est omnia vendere et postea ad suum victum laborare: non ergo mendicare.*

     **En.** Verse 33. — The Gloss, which is the *ordinary* Gloss in Strabo and Lyra, follows Bede (on this place): That, all your things [so also L, which here inverts the order of the arguments] having once been spurned for the Lord etc. — After the words of the Gloss L continues: *therefore it belongs to Christian perfection to sell all things and afterwards to labour for one's own sustenance: therefore not to beg.*

[^p134-8]: **La.** Vers. 12. — Glossa est *ordinaria* apud Strabum et Lyranum: Opus agendum est, non otiandum... et non desiderabitis rem alterius, nedum rogetis etc. — Post Glossam legit L: *sed omnis mendicus rogat sive petit: ergo omnis mendicus facit contra Apostolum; sed qui facit contra Apostolum non est in perfectione christiana*

     **En.** Verse 12. — The Gloss is the *ordinary* Gloss in Strabo and Lyra: Work is to be done, not idling... and you will not desire another's goods, much less ask for them etc. — After the Gloss L reads: *but every beggar asks or requests: therefore every beggar acts against the Apostle; but he who acts against the Apostle is not in Christian perfection*

[^p135-1]: **La.** Vers. 12: Rogamus autem vos, fratres, ut etc. — Glossa *ordinaria* apud Strabum et Lyranum sumta est ex Comment. (in hunc loc.) attributo alias Ambrosio. — L *minorem* exhibet sic: *sed a iustitia declinare non est perfectionis christianae; sed omnis mendicus est egenus: ergo* etc.

     **En.** Verse 12: And we beseech you, brethren, that etc. — The *ordinary* Gloss in Strabo and Lyra is taken from the Commentary (on this place) sometimes attributed to Ambrose. — L gives the *minor* thus: *but to turn aside from justice does not belong to Christian perfection; but every beggar is needy: therefore* etc.

[^p135-2]: **La.** Vers. 10. Vulgata *nec* pro *non.* — Glossa, in qua textus originalis omittit *ut habeant,* est *ordinaria* apud Strabum et Lyranum (qui ipsam attribuit August., de Opere monachor. c. 1. n. 2. seqq.). — L prosequitur post verba Glossae: *ergo omnis mendicus facit contra Apostolum; sed qui facit contra Apostolum non habet perfectionem christianam, ergo nec qui mendicat.*

     **En.** Verse 10. The Vulgate has *nec* for *non.* — The Gloss, in which the original text omits *ut habeant* ("so that they may have"), is the *ordinary* Gloss in Strabo and Lyra (who attributes it to Augustine, *On the Work of Monks*, ch. 1, n. 2 ff.). — L continues after the words of the Gloss: *therefore every beggar acts against the Apostle; but he who acts against the Apostle does not have Christian perfection, therefore neither does he who begs.*

[^p135-3]: **La.** Vers. 9. — Glossa, quam refert Petr. Lombard. et Lyranus ut *ordinariam,* sumta est ex Comment. (in hunc loc.), qui olim Ambrosio tribuebatur. — Post verba Glossae legit L: *sed omnis mendicus frequenter ad alienam mensam accedit: ergo omnis mendicus otio deditus adulatur; sed nullus in perfectione christianae religionis existens adulatur, cum adulatio sit peccatum mortale: ergo nullus mendicus est in perfectione christianae religionis.*

     **En.** Verse 9. — The Gloss, which Peter Lombard and Lyra report as the *ordinary* Gloss, is taken from the Commentary (on this place) which was once attributed to Ambrose. — After the words of the Gloss L reads: *but every beggar frequently comes to another's table: therefore every beggar, given to idleness, flatters; but no one existing in the perfection of the Christian religion flatters, since flattery is a mortal sin: therefore no beggar is in the perfection of the Christian religion.*

[^p135-4]: **La.** Can. 16, qui Urbano quidem adscribebatur, sed alium auctorem habet. — L legit *Item, Causa duodecima quaest. prima, c.* Videntes summi Pontifices, *dicitur, quod nullus communem vitam ducens sit in christiana religione indigens; sed omnis mendicus indiget: ergo nullus mendicus est in perfectione christianae religionis secundum decreta Sanctorum et maxime, quia in fine istius decreti dicitur:* Si aliquis exstiteri .. iam dicta damnatione feriatur. *Ergo omnis, qui mendicus est vel indigens, per istud decretum excommunicatus est, et sic in peccato mortali; sed nullus talis est in statu perfectionis: ergo* etc.

     **En.** Canon 16, which was indeed ascribed to Urban, but has another author. — L reads: *Likewise, Causa 12, question 1, chapter* Videntes summi Pontifices, *it is said that no one leading the common life should be indigent in the Christian religion; but every beggar is indigent: therefore no beggar is in the perfection of the Christian religion according to the decrees of the Saints, and above all because at the end of that decree it is said:* If anyone shall have arisen .. let him be struck with the condemnation already stated. *Therefore everyone who is a beggar or indigent is excommunicated by that decree, and so in mortal sin; but no such person is in the state of perfection: therefore* etc.

[^p135-5]: **La.** Can. *Nemo ecclesiam* (9.). — Pro *consecretur* et *consecrari* I *construatur* et *construi,* quae lectio canoni magis congruit: Nemo ecclesiam aedificet... et ante praefiniat qui aedificare vult, quae ad luminaria et ad custodiam et ad stipendia custodum sufficiant, et ostensa donatione sic domum aedificet etc.

     **En.** Canon *Nemo ecclesiam* (9). — For *consecretur* ("be consecrated") and *consecrari* ("to be consecrated") I has *construatur* ("be built") and *construi* ("to be built"), a reading that agrees better with the canon: Let no one build a church... and let him who wishes to build determine beforehand what will suffice for the lamps and for the custody and for the stipends of the custodians, and, the donation having been shown, let him so build the house etc.

[^p135-6]: **La.** Cod. lib. XI. tit. 25. lege unica *Cunctis quos in publicum,* in Authen. — Superius pro *inhibetur* D E K *prohibetur;* inferius pro *gubernationem* I *ordinationem* et pro *sublimitatem perfectionis* D *perfectionem legis.*

     **En.** *Code*, book XI, title 25, the single law *Cunctis quos in publicum*, in the *Authenticum*. — Above, for *inhibetur* ("it is forbidden") D E K have *prohibetur* ("it is prohibited"); below, for *gubernationem* ("governance") I has *ordinationem* ("ordering"), and for *sublimitatem perfectionis* ("the sublimity of perfection") D has *perfectionem legis* ("the perfection of the law").

[^p135-7]: **La.** D *intercedendum.*

     **En.** D has *intercedendum* ("to intercede").

[^p135-8]: **La.** Vide infra in corp. quaest. — Pro *Item, huiusmodi* D *Item qui.*

     **En.** See below in the body of the question. — For *Item, huiusmodi* ("Likewise, those of this kind") D has *Item qui* ("Likewise, those who").

[^p135-9]: **La.** Cfr. IV. Reg. 5, 20. seqq.

     **En.** Compare IV Kings 5:20 ff.

[^p135-10]: **La.** Epist. I. Thess. 5, 22: Ab omni specie mala abstinete vos. — Post *pseudo-praedicatoribus* K addit *vel quaestuarii.*

     **En.** First Epistle to the Thessalonians 5:22: From every evil appearance refrain yourselves. — After *pseudo-praedicatoribus* ("pseudo-preachers") K adds *vel quaestuarii* ("or questors", i.e. professional alms-collectors).

[^p136-1]: **La.** Edd. perperam *praedicando.*

     **En.** The editions wrongly have *praedicando* ("by preaching").

[^p136-2]: **La.** L pro isto arg. substituit hoc: *Item, persona praedicatoris debet esse discreta et nobilis sive honorabilis, non vilis; sed nulla vilior persona quam persona mendicantis: ergo persona mendicantis, ut praedicatoris, non debet esse mendicans; et sic mendicare praedicantibus non est opus perfectionis christianae.*

     **En.** In place of this argument L substitutes this one: *Likewise, the person of a preacher ought to be discreet and noble or honourable, not vile; but no person is viler than the person of a beggar: therefore the person of a beggar, as of a preacher, ought not to be begging; and so to beg is not, for those who preach, a work of Christian perfection.*

[^p136-3]: **La.** Vers. 9. seq. — Glossa est *interlinearis* apud Lyranum, qua posita, L prosequitur: *Cum ergo necessaria a Domino discipulis gerere inhibeantur, eisdem accipere ab eis quibus praedicant conceditur sive petere; sed hoc est mendicare: ergo Dominus praecepit mendicationem, et ita erit perfectionis christianae pro Christo mendicare, maxime quia illa praecepta sunt non parabolice, sed ad litteram intelligenda.*

     **En.** Verse 9 f. — The Gloss is the *interlinear* Gloss in Lyra; having set it down, L continues: *Since therefore the disciples are forbidden by the Lord to carry necessities, it is granted to them to receive them from those to whom they preach, or to request them; but this is to beg: therefore the Lord commanded begging, and so it will belong to Christian perfection to beg for Christ, above all because those precepts are to be understood not parabolically, but according to the letter.*

[^p136-4]: **La.** Vers. 8: Et praecepit... in via, nisi virgam tantum, non peram etc. — In fine arg. allegatur Matth. 19, 12. — L: *Istud praeceptum* [Marc. 6, 8.] *datum Apostolis aut datur a Domino tanquam praelatis, vel tanquam perfectis. Non tanquam praelatis; quia, si sic, praelati hoc deberent facere: ergo cum non faciant, non datur Apostolis tanquam praelatis; et ita datur eis tanquam perfectis: ergo istud quilibet potest facere perfectus; sed per hoc praeceptum conceditur petitio sumtuum: ergo perfectionis est petere sumtus; hoc est mendicare: ergo* etc.

     **En.** Verse 8: And he commanded... for the way, but a staff only; no scrip etc. — At the end of the argument Matt. 19:12 is alleged. — L: *This precept* [Mark 6:8] *given to the Apostles is given by the Lord either as to prelates, or as to the perfect. Not as to prelates; because, if so, prelates would have to do this: therefore, since they do not do it, it is not given to the Apostles as to prelates; and so it is given to them as to the perfect: therefore anyone who is perfect can do this; but by this precept the requesting of expenses is granted: therefore it belongs to perfection to request expenses; this is to beg: therefore* etc.

[^p136-5]: **La.** Vers. 3. — Glossa est *ordinaria* apud Strabum et Lyranum. — In L desideratur hoc arg.

     **En.** Verse 3. — The Gloss is the *ordinary* Gloss in Strabo and Lyra. — This argument is wanting in L.

[^p136-6]: **La.** Vers. 48. Vulgata post *puerum* addit *istum.* — Glossam *interlinearem* exhibet Lyranus (idem dicitur in Glossa *ordinaria,* sumta ex Beda, apud Strabum et Lyranum: Vel simpliciter *pauperes* Christi... esse honore suscipiendos etc.). — Seq. Glossa est *ordinaria* (ex Ambros., VII. Expos. in Luc. n. 24.) apud Strabum et Lyranum. — Post hanc Glossam L legit: *sed nemo magis pauper quam mendicus: ergo pauperes mendicantes a fidelibus sunt maxime recipiendi; sed non essent a fidelibus recipiendi, si peccatum facerent mendicando: ergo mendicare pro Christo non est peccatum, immo potius perfectionis.*

     **En.** Verse 48. After *puerum* ("child") the Vulgate adds *istum* ("this"). — Lyra gives the *interlinear* Gloss (the same is said in the *ordinary* Gloss, taken from Bede, in Strabo and Lyra: Or simply that the *poor* of Christ... are to be received with honour etc.). — The following Gloss is the *ordinary* Gloss (from Ambrose, *Exposition of Luke* VII, n. 24) in Strabo and Lyra. — After this Gloss L reads: *but no one is more poor than a beggar: therefore poor mendicants are most of all to be received by the faithful; but they would not have to be received by the faithful if they committed sin by begging: therefore to beg for Christ is not a sin, but rather belongs to perfection.*

[^p136-7]: **La.** Vers. 4. — Glossam, quae sumta est ex Beda (in hunc loc.), exhibent Strabus et Lyranus ut *ordinariam.* — L omittit hoc arg.

     **En.** Verse 4. — The Gloss, which is taken from Bede (on this place), Strabo and Lyra give as the *ordinary* Gloss. — L omits this argument.

[^p136-8]: **La.** Vers. 9. — Glossa est *interlinearis* apud Lyranum. — Inferius allegatur Matth. 5, 3: Beati pauperes spiritu, quoniam ipsorum est regnum caelorum. — Post verba Glossae L: *sed isti sunt pauperes spiritu: ergo pauperibus spiritu debent fideles ministrare necessaria, et ab eis necessaria recipere; sed nullus magis pauper spiritu, quam qui et omnibus et proprio et in communi renuntiat: ergo talis potest recipere eleemosynas; et qui potest recipere et petere supplicando potest; sed hoc est mendicare: ergo mendicare non est peccatum, immo potius perfectionis.*

     **En.** Verse 9. — The Gloss is the *interlinear* Gloss in Lyra. — Below, Matt. 5:3 is alleged: Blessed are the poor in spirit, for theirs is the kingdom of heaven. — After the words of the Gloss L: *but these are the poor in spirit: therefore the faithful ought to minister necessities to the poor in spirit, and to receive necessities from them; but no one is more poor in spirit than he who renounces all things, both his own and in common: therefore such a one can receive alms; and he who can receive can also request by supplicating; but this is to beg: therefore to beg is not a sin, but rather belongs to perfection.*

[^p137-1]: **La.** Num. 15: Numquid isti pauperes, inter quorum pannos et illuviem etc. — Allegatur Ps. 40, 1. — L omittit hoc arg. — Circa finem pro *tribuat* fide D E M posuimus *tribuas.*

     **En.** Number 15: Can these poor, amid whose rags and uncleanness etc. — Ps. 40:1 is alleged. — L omits this argument. — Near the end, on the authority of D E M, we have put *tribuas* ("you should give") for *tribuat* ("he should give").

[^p137-2]: **La.** Vers. 14. — Glossa, quae exhibetur a Petro Lombardo (in hunc loc.), est *interlinearis* apud Lyranum. — Inferius respicitur Phil. 4, 15: Nulla mihi ecclesia communicavit in ratione dati et accepti nisi vos soli. — Post Glossam L prosequitur: *ergo illi qui vacant divinis possunt recipere a divitibus; sed pauperes mendicantes pro Christo vacant divinis: ergo tales possunt recipere eleemosynas et etiam petere supplicando, et ita idem quod prius.*

     **En.** Verse 14. — The Gloss, which is given by Peter Lombard (on this place), is the *interlinear* Gloss in Lyra. — Below, Phil. 4:15 is in view: No church communicated with me in the matter of giving and receiving, but you only. — After the Gloss L continues: *therefore those who are free for divine things can receive from the rich; but poor mendicants for Christ are free for divine things: therefore such can receive alms and also request by supplicating, and so the same as before.*

[^p137-3]: **La.** Psalm. 39, 18. — Glossa est *ordinaria* apud Strabum et Lyranum, qui ipsam attribuunt Cassiodoro (in hunc loc.).

     **En.** Ps. 39:18. — The Gloss is the *ordinary* Gloss in Strabo and Lyra, who attribute it to Cassiodorus (on this place).

[^p137-4]: **La.** Psalm. 108, 17. — Glossa exhibetur a Petro Lombardo (in hunc loc.; apud Lyranum ipsa est partim *interlinearis,* partim *ordinaria*) et est secundum Cassiodor. — In L desideratur hoc arg.

     **En.** Ps. 108:17. — The Gloss is given by Peter Lombard (on this place; in Lyra it is partly *interlinear*, partly *ordinary*) and follows Cassiodorus. — This argument is wanting in L.

[^p137-5]: **La.** Vers. 11: Et circumspectis omnibus, cum iam vespera esset hora, exiit etc. — Glossa apud Lyranum *interlinearis* est et secundum Bedam in hunc loc. — L: *Glossa, «Si quis eum hospitio susciperet», circumspiciebat: ergo aut est ut de hospitio, aut ut hospitium conduceret, aut ut sibi hospitium dari vellet. Non primo modo, quod patet per Glossam: «Si quis eum hospitio susciperet». Non secundo modo, per Glossam; ibidem enim: «Tantae paupertatis fuit, ut in tanta urbe nullum hospitium inveniret»; ergo ut volens hospitium sibi dari gratis circumspiciebat, et ita petebat signo et voluntate, sed non verbo; sed sic petere est mendicare: ergo Christus mendicavit, et ita perfecti ipsum sequendo sine peccato possunt mendicare.*

     **En.** Verse 11: And having viewed all things round about, when now the eventide was come, he went out etc. — The Gloss in Lyra is *interlinear* and follows Bede on this place. — L: *The Gloss: «whether anyone would receive him with hospitality», he looked round about: therefore it is either as concerning a lodging, or that he might hire a lodging, or that he wished a lodging to be given him. Not in the first way, as is clear through the Gloss: «whether anyone would receive him with hospitality». Not in the second way, through the Gloss; for in the same place: «He was of such great poverty that in so great a city he found no lodging»; therefore he looked round about as one wishing a lodging to be given him freely, and so he requested by sign and by will, but not by word; but so to request is to beg: therefore Christ begged, and so the perfect, following him, can beg without sin.*

[^p137-6]: **La.** Vers. 5. — Glossa est *interlinearis* apud Lyranum et sumta ex Ambros., VIII. Expos. Evang. secundum Luc. n. 82: Alterum sanaturus interrogat, apud alterum se non invitaturum [alias: *se non invitatus*] invitat. — L post Glossam: *Dominus ergo petiit hospitium: ergo qui sequuntur Christum hoc facere possunt; sed hoc est mendicare: ergo mendicare possunt pro Christo perfecti imitatores Christi.*

     **En.** Verse 5. — The Gloss is the *interlinear* Gloss in Lyra and is taken from Ambrose, *Exposition of the Gospel according to Luke* VIII, n. 82: Being about to heal the one, he questions him; with the other, who would not have invited him, he invites himself [otherwise: *se non invitatus*, "himself, not invited"]. — L after the Gloss: *The Lord therefore requested a lodging: therefore those who follow Christ can do this; but this is to beg: therefore the perfect imitators of Christ can beg for Christ.*

[^p137-7]: **La.** Vers. 7. — Verba August. habentur in Ioan. Evang. tr. 15. n. 2. et n. 12. (textus originalis pro secundo *et promittit* substituit *et affluit*). — Glossa inferius posita apud Strabum et Lyranum est *ordinaria* in Ioan. 4, 7. — In fine arg. pro *facto* I *sibi.*

     **En.** Verse 7. — Augustine's words are in the *Tractates on the Gospel of John*, tr. 15, n. 2 and n. 12 (for the second *et promittit* ("and promises") the original text substitutes *et affluit* ("and abounds")). — The Gloss set down below is, in Strabo and Lyra, the *ordinary* Gloss on John 4:7. — At the end of the argument, for *facto* ("by the deed") I has *sibi* ("for himself").

[^p138-1]: **La.** Sive Epist. 125. (alias 4.) n. 1. Textus originalis in secunda propos. substituit *credidit* pro *credit,* et *cognovit* pro *cognoscit,* pluribus praeterea additis.

     **En.** That is, Epistle 125 (otherwise 4), n. 1. In the second sentence the original text substitutes *credidit* ("believed") for *credit* ("believes"), and *cognovit* ("knew") for *cognoscit* ("comes to know"), several things being added besides.

[^p138-2]: **La.** Cfr. Luc. 7, 36; 11, 37; 14, 1. — Inferius pro *hoc est* D *hoc erat.*

     **En.** Compare Luke 7:36; 11:37; 14:1. — Below, for *hoc est* ("this is") D has *hoc erat* ("this was").

[^p138-3]: **La.** Vers. 37: Circuierunt in etc. — I: *Item, ad Hebr. 11. exemplum Sanctorum laudans Apostolus ait:* Circumierunt etc.

     **En.** Verse 37: They went about in etc. — I: *Likewise, in Hebr. 11, praising the example of the Saints, the Apostle says:* They wandered about etc.

[^p138-4]: **La.** Vers. 4. seqq. — Verba August. inveniuntur in Serm. 11. (alias 18. inter 50 homil.) n. 2. Voci *Ostendit* textus originalis praemittit *Nonne illi panem afferebat corvus mane et ad vesperam carnes?* Subinde pro *quando* exhibet *quomodo.*

     **En.** Verse 4 ff. — Augustine's words are found in Sermon 11 (otherwise 18 among the 50 homilies), n. 2. Before the word *Ostendit* ("showed") the original text puts *Nonne illi panem afferebat corvus mane et ad vesperam carnes?* ("Did not a raven bring him bread in the morning and flesh in the evening?"). Next, for *quando* ("when") it gives *quomodo* ("how").

[^p138-5]: **La.** Ex libro Abdiae, Babyloniae primi episcopi ab Apostolis constituti, de historia certaminis Apostolorum libri X, Iulio Africano interprete etc. Paris. 1566.» lib. V. pag. 62. — L: *Item, in legenda B. Ioannis duo honorifici viri, omnibus suis relictis sive pauperibus erogatis, secuti sunt B. Ioannem; cum non post longum tempus reversi sunt ad civitatem, de qua fuerant, tentati sunt a diabolo, eo quod viderent se egentes; et ita B. Ioannes et ipsi egebant; sed nullus magis est egens quam pauper mendicus pro Christo: ergo tales maxime sunt in statu perfectionis.*

     **En.** From the book of Abdias, first bishop of Babylon, appointed by the Apostles, *On the History of the Contest of the Apostles*, in ten books, translated by Julius Africanus etc., Paris 1566.» book V, page 62. — L: *Likewise, in the legend of blessed John, two honourable men, having left all their things or paid them out to the poor, followed blessed John; when not long afterwards they returned to the city from which they had come, they were tempted by the devil, because they saw themselves in need; and so blessed John and they themselves were in need; but no one is more needy than a poor beggar for Christ: therefore such are most of all in the state of perfection.*

[^p138-6]: **La.** Homil. 4. de Laudibus Pauli (tom. 2. pag. 494. seq.): Audi... ipsum [Paulum, II. Cor. 11, 6.] loquentem: *Etsi imperitus sermone* nec pecunias possidens; nam hoc quoque ipse [I. Cor. 4, 11.] testatur: *Usque* etc.

     **En.** Homily 4 *On the Praises of Paul* (tome 2, page 494 f.): Hear... him [Paul, II Cor. 11:6] saying: *Although I be rude in speech*, and not possessing money; for this too he himself [I Cor. 4:11] testifies: *Even unto* etc.

[^p138-7]: **La.** Cap. 1. — L: *Item, B. Benedictus perfectus fuit, et in II. Dialog. scribitur, quod quidam Romanus in spelunca quadam, ubi orabat, per triennium eum sustentavit, ei necessaria ministrando. Sustentari ergo ab alio, exemplo praedicti Sancti, est perfectionis; sed qui petit eleemosynas pro Christo et accipit ab alio sustentatur: ergo petere ab alio sive mendicare, quod idem est, erit perfectionis.*

     **En.** Chapter 1. — L: *Likewise, blessed Benedict was perfect, and in the second book of the Dialogues it is written that a certain Roman sustained him for three years in a certain cave where he prayed, ministering necessities to him. Therefore to be sustained by another, by the example of the aforesaid Saint, belongs to perfection; but he who requests alms for Christ and receives them is sustained by another: therefore to request from another, or to beg, which is the same, will belong to perfection.*

[^p138-8]: **La.** Vide Breviar. Roman. die 17. Iulii. — L: *Item, B. Alexius, contempto patrimonio, et patre et matre et muliere relictis, ivit per mundum mendicando et Sanctus fuit; sed si mendicare pro Christo esset peccatum, Sanctus non fecisset: ergo exemplo eius viri in statu perfectionis possunt mendicare.*

     **En.** See the Roman Breviary, on 17 July. — L: *Likewise, blessed Alexius, having despised his patrimony and left father and mother and wife, went through the world begging and was a Saint; but if to beg for Christ were a sin, the Saint would not have done it: therefore by his example men in the state of perfection can beg.*

[^p138-9]: **La.** Cfr. lib. II. Annal. Ord. Praedic. n. 24. — L: *Item, hoc videtur exemplo novorum Sanctorum, quod reputo manifestum testimonium, immo magnum quisquis unum repraesentat. Primo exemplo B. Dominici, qui imprecatus est maledictionem in morte ei qui divitias in ordine suo poneret, immo fratres suos voluit per mundum universum mendicare.*

     **En.** Compare book II of the *Annals of the Order of Preachers*, n. 24. — L: *Likewise, this is seen by the example of the new Saints, which I reckon a manifest testimony, indeed a great one, whoever represents one. First by the example of blessed Dominic, who at his death imprecated a curse on anyone who should place riches in his order; indeed he willed his brothers to beg through the whole world.*

[^p138-10]: **La.** Cap. 6. — De praecedentibus cfr. Legenda S. Francisci scripta a S. Bonav. c. 9. — Superius post *consuluit* D addit *et mendicare docuit.* — L: *Item, dicunt: Sanctus Franciscus istud miraculis confirmavit, quia, cum vellet transferri ultra mare, nautae, quia non habebant victum, ipsum recipere noluerunt; et petiit eleemosynas et habuit parum ad victum. Postea ingressus navem cum aliis, cum navigarent per mare, defecerunt victualia aliorum, victus autem praedicti Sancti supererat, unde alios sustentavit; et ita Dominus voluit et fecit, ut alii de mendicatione sustentarentur per miraculum, et mendicatio per miraculum fuit approbata.*

     **En.** Chapter 6. — On what precedes, compare the *Legend of St. Francis* written by St. Bonaventure, ch. 9. — Above, after *consuluit* ("counselled") D adds *et mendicare docuit* ("and taught to beg"). — L: *Likewise, they say: Saint Francis confirmed this by miracles, for, when he wished to be carried across the sea, the sailors, because they had no food, refused to take him; and he requested alms and had a little for food. Afterwards, having boarded a ship with others, while they were sailing over the sea, the provisions of the others failed, but the food of the aforesaid Saint was left over, from which he sustained the others; and so the Lord willed and brought it about that others were sustained by begging through a miracle, and begging was approved through a miracle.*

[^p139-1]: **La.** Cap. 7, 3. — Gregor., VIII. Moral. c. 8. n. 15. — In L desideratur hoc arg.

     **En.** Chapter 7:3. — Gregory, *Morals* VIII, ch. 8, n. 15. — This argument is wanting in L.

[^p139-2]: **La.** Quae nunc est 118. ad Iulian. (alias 34.). n. 5.

     **En.** Which is now 118, to Julian (otherwise 34), n. 5.

[^p139-3]: **La.** Vide tom. II. pag. 760, nota 6. et tom. III. pag. 388, nota 2. — Finem arg. exhibet D sic: *ergo si esse pauperem pro Christo bonum est, et mendicare.* L omittit hoc arg.

     **En.** See tome II, page 760, note 6, and tome III, page 388, note 2. — D gives the end of the argument thus: *therefore, if to be poor for Christ is good, so also is to beg.* L omits this argument.

[^p139-4]: **La.** Aristot., II. Ethic. c. 3: Circa id quod difficilius est semper ars et virtus versatur.

     **En.** Aristotle, *Ethics* II, ch. 3: Art and virtue are always concerned with what is more difficult.

[^p139-5]: **La.** D *meritorium et satisfactorium,* et inferius vocibus *se astringit* interserit *obligat et.*

     **En.** D has *meritorium et satisfactorium* ("meritorious and satisfactory"), and below it inserts *obligat et* ("obliges and") into the words *se astringit* ("binds himself").

[^p139-6]: **La.** M *maxime.*

     **En.** M has *maxime* ("most of all").

[^p139-7]: **La.** D E M *altari,* sed contra contextum.

     **En.** D E M have *altari* ("the altar"), but against the context.

[^p139-8]: **La.** Duo seqq. argg. omittuntur a L, qui in hoc arg. addit: &nbsp;&nbsp;*t haec argumenta sufficiant ad istius partis ostensionem.*

     **En.** The two following arguments are omitted by L, which in this argument adds: &nbsp;&nbsp;*t* [*sic*] *let these arguments suffice for the showing of this side.*

[^p139-9]: **La.** Prov. 19, 17: Feneratur Domino qui miseretur pauperis etc.

     **En.** Prov. 19:17: He that hath mercy on the poor, lendeth to the Lord etc.

[^p140-1]: **La.** Vers. 20. et 22: Factum est autem, ut moreretur mendicus et portaretur ab Angelis etc.

     **En.** Verses 20 and 22: And it came to pass that the beggar died and was carried by the Angels etc.

[^p140-2]: **La.** Vide II. Homil. in Evang. homil. 36, in qua explicat parabolam de coena magna, ad quam multi erant vocati, qui tamen «coeperunt omnes simul excusare... Et ait Dominus servo: Exi... et *compelle intrare*» [Luc. 14, 16 — 24.]. Ultima haec verba loc. cit. n. 9. explicat de adversitatibus compellentibus ad Deum redire: «Qui ergo huius mundi adversitatibus fracti, ad Dei amorem redeunt atque a praesentis vitae desideriis corriguntur, quid isti, fratres mei, nisi compelluntur, ut intrent»? — L, ad solutionem quaestionis triplici posita mendicationis distinctione, scil. «ex necessitate naturae, ex vitiositate culpae, ex supererogatione iustitiae», de primo membro affert tantum exemplum Lazari, et etiam de modo secundo pauca refert.

     **En.** See the second book of the *Homilies on the Gospels,* homily 36, in which he explains the parable of the great supper, to which many had been called, who nevertheless «began all at once to make excuse... And the Lord said to the servant: Go out... and *compel them to come in*» [Luke 14:16–24]. These last words he explains in the place cited, n. 9, of the adversities that compel a man to return to God: «Those, then, who, broken by the adversities of this world, return to the love of God and are corrected from the desires of the present life — what are they, my brethren, but compelled to enter»? — L, at the solution of the question, having set down the threefold distinction of begging, namely «from the necessity of nature, from the viciousness of fault, from the supererogation of justice», brings forward for the first member only the example of Lazarus, and reports few things also about the second mode.

[^p140-3]: **La.** Libr. II. c. 16. n. 76.

     **En.** Book II, ch. 16, n. 76.

[^p140-4]: **La.** Cap. 25. n. 33. — Pro *deliciis* edd. cum aliquot codd. *divitiis.*

     **En.** Ch. 25, n. 33. — For *deliciis* ("delights") the editions, with several codices, have *divitiis* ("riches").

[^p140-5]: **La.** Cap. 28. n. 36. Respicitur Matth. 23, 5, ubi de Scribis et Pharisaeis dicitur: Dilatant enim phylacteria sua et magnificant fimbrias.

     **En.** Ch. 28, n. 36. Reference is made to Matt. 23:5, where it is said of the Scribes and Pharisees: For they make broad their phylacteries and enlarge their fringes.

[^p140-6]: **La.** Matth. 6, 25, seqq.: Ideo dico vobis, ne solliciti sitis animae vestrae quid manducetis... Respicite volatilia caeli, quoniam non serunt etc. — Verbum Apostoli est II. Thess. 3, 10: Si quis non vult operari, nec manducet.

     **En.** Matt. 6:25 ff.: Therefore I say to you, be not solicitous for your life, what you shall eat... Behold the birds of the air, for they neither sow etc. — The word of the Apostle is II Thess. 3:10: If any man will not work, neither let him eat.

[^p140-7]: **La.** Cap. 22. seq. n. 26. seq. Post *misericordiam* textus originalis plura addit et circa finem pro *ut impedire velint Evangelio* substituit *ut impediri velint Evangelio.* Correximus vitium codd. et edd., qui habent *impedire velint Evangelium.*

     **En.** Ch. 22 f., n. 26 f. After *misericordiam* ("mercy") the original text adds several things, and towards the end for *ut impedire velint Evangelio* it substitutes *ut impediri velint Evangelio* ("that they wish the Gospel to be hindered"). We have corrected the fault of the codices and editions, which have *impedire velint Evangelium.*

[^p141-1]: **La.** Serm. 11. (alias 18. inter 50 homilias n. 1.). Respicitur Luc. 16, 9: Facite vobis amicos de mammona etc. Textus originalis: In isto autem tempore, ut noverimus, quantum... fecit, ut, cum fiunt hic amici de mammona etc. [subinde textus originalis aliter legit, in nota adiecta exhibetur tamen lectio nostra].

     **En.** Sermon 11 (otherwise 18, among the 50 homilies, n. 1). Reference is made to Luke 16:9: Make unto you friends of the mammon etc. The original text reads: But in this time, that we might know, how much... he made, that, when men are made friends here of the mammon etc. [thereafter the original text reads otherwise; in an added note our reading is nevertheless given].

[^p141-2]: **La.** L etiam de tertio modo multa omittens et male contrahens agit. — Vocibus *laudabilis est* D praemittit *meritoria et.*

     **En.** L treats of the third mode too, omitting much and abridging it badly. — Before the words *laudabilis est* ("is praiseworthy") D puts *meritoria et* ("meritorious and").

[^p141-3]: **La.** Alias 33. n. 4. seq. Pro *qui docebantur* (τῶν μαθητευομένων) edd. *qui docebant,* et pro *monstrat hoc debitum existens* (δείκνυσι τὸ πρᾶγμα ὀφειλὴν ὄν) eaedem edd. *monstrat hoc debitum existere,* ac demum *a nobis* pro *a vobis* (παρ' ὑμῶν).

     **En.** Otherwise 33, n. 4 f. For *qui docebantur* ("who were being taught", τῶν μαθητευομένων) the editions have *qui docebant* ("who were teaching"), and for *monstrat hoc debitum existens* ("showing this to be a debt", δείκνυσι τὸ πρᾶγμα ὀφειλὴν ὄν) the same editions have *monstrat hoc debitum existere,* and finally *a nobis* ("from us") for *a vobis* ("from you", παρ' ὑμῶν).

[^p141-4]: **La.** Psalm. 103, 14. Verba allegata habentur in Glossa *ordinaria* apud Strabum, Petr. Lombard. et Lyranum (in hunc loc.) et formata sunt ex iis quae August. (Enarrat. in Ps. 103. serm. 3. n. 9. seq.) docet. — Loci Scripturae sunt I. Cor. 9, 14: Ita et Dominus ordinavit iis qui Evangelium annuntiant de Evangelio vivere. Secundus ibid. v. 19; tertius ibid. v. 9, sumtus ex Deut. 25, 4; quartus Luc. 10, 7: Dignus est enim operarius etc. Quintus I. Cor. 9, 11: Si nos vobis spiritualia seminavimus, magnum est, si nos carnalia vestra metamus? Sextus Ps. 40, 1; septimus Luc. 6, 30: Omni autem petenti te tribue. Pro octavo in ed. Lyrani allegatur Eccli. 29. — Textus originalis omittit *ut de Evangelio vivant qui Evangelium annuntiant,* et post *humiles* plura addit.

     **En.** Ps. 103:14. The words cited are found in the *ordinary* Gloss in Strabo, Peter Lombard and Lyra (on this passage), and are formed out of what Augustine teaches (*Enarrationes in Psalmos,* on Ps. 103, sermon 3, n. 9 f.). — The Scripture passages are I Cor. 9:14: So also the Lord ordained that they who preach the Gospel should live by the Gospel. The second is ibid. v. 19; the third ibid. v. 9, taken from Deut. 25:4; the fourth Luke 10:7: For the labourer is worthy etc. The fifth I Cor. 9:11: If we have sown unto you spiritual things, is it a great matter if we reap your carnal things? The sixth Ps. 40:1; the seventh Luke 6:30: And give to every one that asketh thee. For the eighth, in Lyra's edition Ecclus. 29 is cited. — The original text omits *ut de Evangelio vivant qui Evangelium annuntiant,* and after *humiles* adds several things.

[^p141-5]: **La.** L omittit quae sequuntur.

     **En.** L omits what follows.

[^p142-1]: **La.** Regul. iuris in VI. regula 53: Cui licet quod est plus, licet utique quod est minus.

     **En.** *Rules of law* in the Sext, rule 53: He to whom the greater is lawful, to him assuredly the lesser is lawful.

[^p142-2]: **La.** Epist. I. Cor. 8, 1: Scientia inflat, caritas vero aedificat. — Aliquanto superius pro *securitatem* I *saturitatem,* et *licite petere, ut proficiat* pro *licite mendicare pro amore proficiendi.*

     **En.** Epistle I Cor. 8:1: Knowledge puffeth up, but charity edifieth. — Somewhat above, for *securitatem* ("security") I has *saturitatem* ("satiety"), and *licite petere, ut proficiat* ("lawfully to request, that he may advance") for *licite mendicare pro amore proficiendi* ("lawfully to beg for love of advancing").
